and three-twelfths to the preceding government fiscal year. The year
into whieh expenditure falls is determined by the date of payment,
the date of fulfillment of a contract (where disbursements are based
on contracts), or the yeer scheduled for payment (in the case of fixed
paymants suoh as pensions, grants, allowanoes, eto.).
4. Budget.
a.) Compilatien and approval. Receipts and expenditures
of the Dmperial Household are planned in advance by an annual budget
system. Each year budgetary estimates for the succeeding year are
made by the Assigned Accounts Offioials and the Bureau or Division
Chiefs in Charge of Special Accounts. Upon the basis of these esti-
mates, the Chief of the Imperial Treasury Bureau (Haiso-to) draws up
the main budget for all accounts except the Main Fund Section and the
Fund for Reserve Supplies of the Imperial Capital Aocounts. This
budget is submitted before October 31 to the Minister of the Imperial
Housebold, who sends it immediately to the Budget Committee which in
turn is roquired to approve or disapprove the items listed within 15
days. The budget is then submitted to the Faperor by Deoember 31,
and, after the daliberation of the Imperial Household Economy Council
receives Imperial sanotion. Supplementary budgets are drawm up and
approved in much the same manner as the necessity for them arises.
b.) Balancing the booke and auditing. At the and of the
fiseal year, the Chief of the Imperial Treasury Bureau drafts the
settlement or balance sheet for those revemues and expenditures pro-
vided for by the budget for the year past, and also presents a balancs
shoet for the accounts not included in the budget. These drafts, ao-
sempamied by monthly and annual statements of receipts and disburse-
meats made by the Assigned Accounts Officials, the Ofricials in charge
of Reoeipt and Disbursement of Funds, and the Managing Directors in
ebarge of special accounts, are submitted with relevamd dooumants by
Angust 10 to the Emperor, at which time the Board of Imperial Auditors
is notified. The latter are charged with finding losses of specie, bul-
lien, ourrenoy or negotiable instruments and fixing responsibility on
the afficials cencerned.
o.) Fore of the budget. The budgots for the Ordinary and
Special Accounts are divided into two sections, a working budget and

an emergenoy budget, and contain preambles explaining the year's ac-
counts and comparing them with the preceding year. Provision for
two reserve funds are made in each of these budgets. The First Re-
serve Fund is intended to supplement unavoidable deficiencies in items
listed in the budget while the Second Reserve Fund provides for neces-
sary expenses arising outside the budget. Items of expenditure in the
First Reserve Fund are designated by the Minister of the Imperial House-
hold at the beginning of the fiscal year. All other emergency expendi-
tures are handled by the Second Reserve Fund, or where it is insufficient,
by supplementary budgets.
5. Receipt and Disbursement of Funds. Subject to the super-
vision of the Imperial Household Minister, the custody, receipt and
disbursement of funds are managed by the Chief of the Imperial Treasury
Bureau, his appointees (particularly the Officials in charge of Receipt
and Disbursement of Funds), and banks or trustees designated by him.
Detailed forms and procedures to be used by them in transactions in-
volving the expenditure or receipt of funds are laid down by the Im-
perial Household Accounting Ordinance and the Rules for the Application
of the Imperial Household Accounting Ordinance.
a.) Receipts. Notices of payment due are sent to the payor
ten days in advance. Payments must generally be made in specie or post-
al savings transfers to the Official in charge of Receipt and Disburse-
ment of Funds or to a designated deposit bank. Receipts are issued by
such officials or by the Chief of the Imperial Treasury Bureau. (In
1928, the Bank of Japan was designated as the official deposit bank
for the Imperial Household).
b.) Disbursements. Except in special instances, disburse-
ments are made in the form of checks drawn upon a deposit bank by the
Chief of the Imperial Treasury Bureau, the Managing Director or the
Assigned Accounts Official concerned. Funds may be transferred to a
local bank on which a check may be drawn for the convanience of the
payee. In every case a receipt must be collected or a disbursement
affidavit made out by the official concerned.
c.) Advance payments. Although generally disburaements
are permitted only after goods have been delivered, contracts falfilled
or work performed, limited exceptions are made to this rule when circum-
stances warrant, e.g., expenses paid before an Imperial visit, travel
expenses, tuition and allowances for persons ordered abroad on official
missions or for study.
d.) Borrowing. Loans from outside sources may be made
with Imperial Sanction after consultation with the Imperial Household
Economic Council. Loans among the various Imperial Household accounts
require no such sanction but must be paid back within the year.

C. Records
Records other than accounts relating to the property of the

Building plans of other buildings may be found in the Superintendance

3. Hereditary Property Recorde are kept in the Archives Section
(Tosho-ka), Archives Bureau (Tosho-ryo), Imperial Palace, Tokyo.
4. Recorde of stocks of bullion or specie on deposit in the
Bank of Japan are probably kept in the headquarters of the Bank of
Japan in Tokyo.

V.
PROBLEMS AND COURSES OF ACTION
Necessity for Military Government Control of Dmperial
Household Property.
In order to prevent activity inimical to the security of the
Military Government and the occupying forces on the part of the Im-
perial Household, its officials or employees; in order to insure
the final disposition of Imperial Household properties (in accordance
with policy to be determined later); in order to facilitate the
utilization of such properties for furthering the immediate pur
poses of the Military Government; and in order to preserve works
of art and other cultural materials, it will be necessary for the
Military Government to place Imperial Household property under its
control.
1. Preventing hostile activi:8
The vast extent and value of the crown fortune wakes it parti-
cularly susceptible of use in a manner prejudicial or hostile to the
occupying forces and the Military Government. of its estimated total
value of about a billion yen, perhaps as much as several hundred mil-
lion yen will be in the form of bullion, specie and currenoy or ne-
gotiable instruments and other liquid assets readily convertible to
cash. It is the direct concern of the Military Government to prevent
the use of such assets to finance the activities of hostile groups,
whether political or military in nature. Furthermore, the Imperial
Household, through its shareholdings in certain Japanese companies
and banks, may occasionally be in a position to use its voting pri-
vileges to obstruct special Military Government policies. This is
particularly true in the case of the Yokohama Specie Bank and the
Japan Mail Steamahip Line (Nippon Yusen Kaisha), avan where special
MG controls are set up over such companies.
2. Insuring final disposition
For the reason that the maintenauce of immense wealth in the hands
of the Imperial Household may very well hinder the democratization
of Japan and for the purpose of utilizing crown properties for mpara-
tions or restitution, the nationalization, transfer or other disposi-
tion of the Imperial Household properties may ultimately be required.
In the absence of any clear statement of policy on this subjoct and
for the period of the belligerent military occupation of Japan,
Military Government will not be expected to implement final dimposi-
tion of the property of the Imperial Household. When such policy
is stated final disposition of the Imperial Household properties
will be in order.
Until such time,the principal duty of the Military Government
will be the prevention of the alienation, secret or "above-board",
or the destruction of Imperial Household properties. This will be
particularly true with regard to liquid assets of which no public
16 008 P38 0u

=== Page 114 === (confidence 0.99)
-29
record has been made and of which little is known at present. In the
very first period after occupstion, it is most likely that the Im-
perial Household, its officiais or agents, will attempt to conceal
such assets in order to avcid the possibility of eventual fines,
penalties or seisures. I's will be the responsibility of the Mili-
tary Government to keep such properties intact and available for
future disposition so far as possible.
3. Furthering the purposes of MG
Particularly with respect to forests, farms, livestock and motor
vehicles, it may be necessary for the Military Government to utilise
Imperial Household property to carry out its programs and further the
military administration of Japan. For example, in the event that
lumber is needed as building material for military purposes, use of
the resources of the crown forests might be required. It will be es-
sential that all peoductive and usable facilities needed in connection
with these programs be administered or managed by perscns known to
be willing to cooperate in attaining MG objectives.
4. Protecting Works of Art and other cultural materials.
In the event that Imperial palaces, axhibition halls or estates
are abandoned, and in view of the fact that such abandoned property
is specially liable to damage or looting, it will be a responsibility
of MG to secure such property in order to preserve law and order
and prevent pillage. Such protection, however, should not have
priority over the security of certain other classes of property
such as those owmed by United Nations nationals, but is espocially
indicated where such works of art may be used for the ultimate
purpose of restitution or compensation.
B.
Methods of Control
In general, two effective methods of control of crown proper-
ties are available to MG:
1. Splitting up of crown properties by category with management of
each category and personnel appertaining thereto transferred from
the Imperial Household Department to the suitable national government
departments.
This method would divorce property control from policy con-
cerning the status of the Emperor and his high officials, and would re-
move crown properties from the furisdiction of officials who might
reasonably be expected to be particularly hostile to MG and loyal
to the Imperial House. It also would help prevent the reconstitution
of the wealth of the Imperial Household after the termination of oc-
cupation. However, it would entail considerable administrative
difficulty and complexity, at least initially, and might interfere
with disposition of the property if policy to that effect were to be
applied during the period of MC.
16 008 P30 bu

=== Page 115 === (confidence 0.99)
-30
2. Management of Imperial Household properties as a unit through the
Imperlal Household Department.
This method is most convenient because it would retain the old,
rather efficisnt organisstion with a minimum of administrative shifts
and with a direct line of authority. It would also make disposition
of crown property a relatively simple matter at any time. However,
the retention of the old organization intact carries with it a serious
danger of sabotage of MG objectives by the old officials (particularly
those appointed by the Emperor), who might be expected to be loyal
to the Emperor and hostile to MG. Effective control of the Department
can be established only through the substitution of an MG offioer or
officers for the Imperial Household Minister and Vice-inister and by
a thorough-going removal of all doubtful personnel from positions of
authority within the Department. On the assumption that such actions
are in accord with over-all policy, this method is recommended.
C. Degree of Supervision
While the MG authorities will be expected to take over control
of the crown properties, the extent and varied nature of the properties
will make it impossible for detailed supervision to be carried on over
all categories of crown properties by MG officers. Generally speaking,
crown properties will fall into four classes, each requiring a different
degree of supervision by MG, as follows:
1. Bullion, specie, currency, negotiable instruments, and other in-
tangibles are liquid assets and may be easily availed of for promoting
activities hostile to MG. They are also easily transferred fraudulently
or clandestinely to "dummies" or otherwise concealed to evade possible
eventual seizure ard disposition of United Nations authorities. On
the other hand, they present no managerial problems. Such propertias
should therefore be seized and turned over to the financial section
of MG.
2. Forests, farms, livestock and productive estates will present
primarily a problem of management for the purposes of maintaining the
value of the properties and utilizing them most fully in connection with
other MG programs. Since such properties cannot be easily utilised
to support hostile activities they will not require detailed MG
supervision, and since they are not particularly susceptible to pil-
lage, no special MG protection will be needed. As a rule, it will
be most desirable to leave the management of such properties to Japanese
officials subject to the direction of MG officers.
3. Living quarters, furnishings and personal property needed for the
day-to-day existence of the Emperor and his immediate family.
This category of crown property will be quite small in amount and
will not be specially liable to looting or susceptible to hostile use.
Neither will it be of any particular use in MG programs. It will,
therefore, require no outside management and might quite safely be
left to the management and use of the Imperial family within the
10-008 P40bu

=== Page 116 === (confidence 0.98)
-31-
limits imposed by MG policy towards the mperor and his immediate
family.
4. Liuseums, exhibition halls and abandoned palaces and estates will
require no control other than sealing and guarding.
D. Recommended Program
1. Appointment of a Crown Property Custodian
a. The value, oxtent and variety of the crom. properties, as
well as the importance of controllin. them effectively, make it
necessary to centralize all responsibility for control of such pro-
perties in one office. Therefore, a Crown Property Custodian should
be appointed from the staff of the Military Governrent to exercise
general control of the properties of the Imperial Household. If an
G officer is placed in charge of the Imperial Household Department
as a whole, he might well serve concurrently as Crown Froperty Cus-
todian or else exercise general supervision over the Crown Property
Custodian. In any event, the actions of the Crown Proverty Custodian
should be in conformity with general G policy towards the Imperial
Housenold.
b. In general, the Crown Pronerty Custodian should actively
aassume all the former functions of the Emperor and the  inister of
the Imperial Household with respect to crown property. Le should be
the representative of the crown properties in legal action and his
duties should include the promulgation of such detailed measures
as will be necessary to apply the Military Government property
control program, the maintenance of records, and the receipt and
inspection of accounts, balance sheets and such other reports as
might be required from Japanese officials appointed by
c. It may be desirable for the Crown Property Custodian to vest
title to the crown properties. Such vesting would facilitate the
mana oment of the crown properties by enabling the Crown Froperty
Custodian to dispose of properties when circumstances so dictate,
.g., when maintenance costs of property are too high, wnen retention
is otherwise uneconomical, or when needed to pay the salaries of employees
engaged in managing or otherwise working on the properties. Vesting
would automatically prevent any subsequent alienation of property
or withdrawal of bank deposits without the specific direction of
the Crowm Property Custodian and would therefore make blocking
procedures unnecessary. If it is not considered necessary to vest
titlo, the Crown Property Custodian should assume a "custodial"
title so that transfers might be made when dictated by considera-
tions of sound management.
d. The rown Property Custodian should have the authority to sus-
pend and amend such portions of the Imperial Household Property Ordi-
nance (Koshitsu Zaisan-rei), the Imperial Household Acoounting Ordinance
1 001 41 b

=== Page 117 === (confidence 0.99)
-32-
(Koshitsu Kaikei-rei), the Rules for the Application of the Imperial
Household Accounting Ordinance (Koshitsu Kaikei-rei Shiko Kisoku), and
such other ministerial ordinances as may be necessary to apply
effective controls.
e. The Crowm Property Custodian should set up a regular liaison
with other MG agencies to facilitate cooperation in MG programs other
than that of property control.
2. Invalidation of transfers
a. After vesting of title to the Imperial Household properties by
the Crowm Property Custodian, no subsequent transfers of title would be
valid unless made by the Crowm Property Custodian. However, if it is de-
cided not to vest title in the Crowm Property Custodian, all Imperial
Household property, including bank accounts, will have to be blocked
except for transactions licensed by the Crowm Property Custodian.
b. All transfers of title which were made prior to vesting and
which took effect on or after 7 December 194l may be presumed to have been
made for the purpose of evading or contravening MG controls and should
be declared invalid, unless they were made for the purchase of necessi-
ties of life for the Emperor and the Court, for salary payments to regular
employees of the Imperial Household Department or for custonary property
maintenance.
c. With the same exceptions, all transfers of title to property
in the yet unoccupied zone made subsequent to assumption of control
be declared invalid.
d. All tranafers, even those taking effect prior to 7 December 1941,
should be declared invalid if made for the purpose of evading or defeat-
ing MG controls.
3. Inventory of Imperial Household Properties
a. The description of crowm properties given in this report is neces-
sarily incomplete, out-of-date and in insufficient detail. As soon as
possible, therefore, Imperial Household properties in the occupied
areas should be listed. This listing need not be in great detail at
first but should be made for the purpose of indicating the properties
over which MG control will be placed. As the zone of occupation ax-
pands, additional crwon properties should be added. As soon as con-
venient, a detailed listing should be obtained or compiled. The
customary records and accounts, which are indicated in Chapter IV above,
may be helpful.
b. As more detailed listings are possible, all properties bearing
prima facie evidence of having been looted from China,or other areas
occupied by Japan, should be sequestered.
10 008 P42 bu

=== Page 118 === (confidence 0.99)
-33-
4. Removal of untrustworthy officials
As has been indicated above, the control of Crown properties
through a relatively intact Imperial Household Department organisation,
would confirm inlarge measure the authority of the incunbent officials
of the Department. Since such officials will probably be loyal to the
Imperial House and reluctant to harm its interests through certain of
the MG property control measures, close supervision of their activities
will be essential to the attainment of the MG objectives. But, however
close such supervision might be, the advantages conveyed to hostile
officials by their familiarity with the workings of the department and
the contimuance of their authority over their subordinates will make it
possible for them to intrigue against  G controls. It is therefore
recommended that those officials who by their past reoords are most
likely to be hostile to MG or especially loyal to the Imperial House
should be ruled ineligible to hold positions of authority with respect
to crown properties. It is suggested that the following persons be
considered ineligible:
a. All Japanese who actively participated, in a responsible
capacity, in the formulation or execution of Japanese imperialistic
policies prior to 7 December 194l, or in the prosecution of the Pacific
War since that date.
b.. All appointees of the Emperor himself, i.e., those holding shin-
nin or chokmin rank. As of July 1, 1939, there were 94 officials
(4 shinnin and 70 chokunin)of such rank holding office in the Imperial
Household Department, but only ten of these held positions of authority
with respect to crom properties: the Imperial Household Minister,
the Imperial Household Vice-Minister, one Counselor (Sanji-kan) of the
Winistry, the Chief of the Imperial Treasury Bureau, the Chief of the
Imperial Maws Buresu, the Chief of the Board of Imperial Anditors, and
the Chief, the Chief Secretary and two Technical Experts (gishi) of the
Imperial Forestry and Estates Bureau.
5.
Civil List
a. The Civil List was voted the Emperor for the maintenance of
the Imperial Court and the Imperial Household. On the assemption that
the Imperial Court, together w th its ritualh and functions, will be
suspanded during MG, and since the major portion of the Imperial
Household expenses have been incurred through the management of crowm
properties which will be under the control of MG and not theEmperor,
i is recommended that the Civil List be terminated. The day-to-day
living expenses of the Emperor and his immediate family, however,
should be considered as a legitimate governmental expense to be
paid out of governmental rovenues.
b. The pre-war income from the crown properties was more than
adequate to pay for their maintenance and for the salaries of the regular
employees of the Imperial Household Department who managed them.
18-008 P43 bu

=== Page 119 === (confidence 0.99)
-34-
The proceeds of the crown properties, therefore, should be used to pay
for such maintenance and salaries during the period of MG.
6.
Bullion, apecie, currency, negotiable instruments and other
intangibles
a. All stocks of gold and silver bullion, specie, currency and
all realizable securities, including corporate stocks, short-term
commercial paper and national, prefectural,municipal and corporate
bonds, should be seized, listed and turned over to the financial
section of MG, with the exception of such liquid assets as are neces-
sary to administer the crown forests, farms, stock farms and other crown
properties which are to be operated or maintained under Military Govern-
ment.
b. Lists of bullion, specie, currency and intangibles owmed by
the Imperial Household together with their location may be found in the
Imperial Capital Account or other records kept by the Imperial Treasury
Bureau. Records of short-term commercial paper will also probably be
found in the Ordinary Account kept by the Imperial Treasury Bureau.
The Bank of Japan, as the official deposit bank for the Imperial
Household, may have been custodian of a good part of the bullion,
specie and currency, and in the course of the reorganisation of the
Bank of Japan by MG, such stocks will probably be registered.l
c. No cash should be paid out, except that for salaries of regu-
lar Imperial Household Department employees and for maintenance of pro-
perties.
7. Productive forest and farms
a. Management of forests, farms and livestock should be contimued
in the Imperial Household Forestry and Estates Bureau, as is or in som
modified form, under the supervision of the Crowm Property Custodian.
This Bureau was relatively autonomous within the Imperial Household
Department and it may easily be utilized by the Crown Property Custodian
as a unit. To insure effective supervision, it may be desirable to
appoint an MG officer to take charge of this Bureau under the supervision
of the Crowm :roperty Custodian.
b. Scme of the crowm forests are likely to fall into MG hands
before crown property in any other categories, particularly if the occu-
pation of Japan proceeds from the north. If, as is likely, crown
forests come under MG control before the central office of the Imperial
1
See Civil Affairs Guide: Control and Use of The Bank of Japan during
The Period of Military Government.
10-008 P44 bu

=== Page 120 === (confidence 0.99)
-35-
Household Forestry and Estates Burean can be taken over, some of the
five branch offices and the sixty-three stations may be available to
MG for management purposes. As a rule, it will probably be most con-
venient for the Crown Property Custodian to work through the highest
administrative office of the Bureau available to him. If two or more
branch officer are in the occupied sone, the Crowm Property Custodian
might set up a skeleton central office to be combined with the regular
central Bureau office when the latter falls under MG control.
c. In most cases, the customary management procedures should be
continued. For security reasons and for the convenience of the Crown
Property Custodian, all communications, including reports and directives,
between stations, branches and the central Bureau office should be made
in parallel Japanese and English texts and duplicates filed with the
Crown Property Custodian.
d. Through a regular liaison between the Crown Property Custodian
and other MG agencies concerned, the crown forests should be enlisted
in any production program deemed essential by MG to the security of the
occupying forces or the tranquility of the civil population. It is
worthy of note that a similar arrangement between the Imperial Household
Department and the Department of Agriculture and Commerce has already
been used with success by the Japanese.
e. While little is known about the crown farms, on the basis of
other Japanese experience, it is likely that they are operated inde-
pendently by tenants with a minimum of superviwion or assistance by the
Imperial Household. Consequently, few problems of direct farm
management may be expected to face the Crown Property Custodian, whose
duties will probably be restricted largely to the collection of rents.
It is possible that temporary dislocation due to the war, sucii as lack
of fertilizer, breakdowm of irrigation, interference with cultivation
by military movements, the obligation of the peasantry to harbor home-
less relatives from devasted urban areas, etc. may compel some tenants
to forego payment of the customary rent. Since eviction of the tenant
under such conditions would interfere with the production of food and
since such eviction might well contribute to agrarian unrest, it is
recommended that evictions from crown lands for non-payment of rent be
suspended until norma? conditions are restored. This would be con-
sistent with customary Japanese practise which is to evict the tenant
only after it became clear that he will be unable to pay his ac-
cumulated back rent at any foreseeable time in the future. Rents col-
lected beyond the amount needed for administration should be transferred
to the MG finance section.
f. Stock-farms will probably be handed most conveniently in the
same manner as the forests.
8.
Palaces, estates, museums, and exhibition halls
As indicated above, the amall part of these properties in actual
use by the Emperor and his immediate family as living quarters might be
10-008 P45 00

=== Page 121 === (confidence 0.99)
-36-
left to the management of the Emperor. All others should be subjected
to a quick inventory when convenient and then sealed. The necessary
police measures should be taken to prevent pillage. If necessary the
pelaces and estates might be made available for billeting of military
forces.
9. Motor vehicles and similar facilities
All transportation facilities should be reported immediataly by the
Chief or the highest remaining official of the Imperial Maws Bureau to
the Crowm Property Custodian. These facilities are subject to seisure.
They may be of use in assisting military movements and should be made
available to the occupying forces as soon as possible.
10. Accounting and Records
a. A notice forbidding destruction or removal of records and
accounts relative to Imperial Household properties should be issued. 
b. As a result of measures taken by MG with respect to Imperial
Household properties, the accounting thereof will have to be modified:
1. Following the seizure of all bullion, specie, currency and
intangibles and their transfer to the financial section of MG, the Imperial
Capital Account will no lcnger be necessary and might well be closed.
All the items in the Main Fund section of the account will have been
seized, and income and disbursements relative thereto will have dis-
appeared. Any income from crown forests and farms normally transferred to
the Receipts and Disbursements section of the Account might be kept in
the Special Forestry and Estates Account or transferred to the financial
section of MG. The Fund for Reserve Supplies might be transferred to
the Ordinary Account.
ii. The Ordinary Account should be retained to keep accurate
record of the remaining crowm properties not kept in special aocounts.
iii. Certain of the Spacial Accounts might be closed for
the period of occupation if the relevant properties are sealed by .
iv. Other of the Special Acoounts should be retained for
institutions which may be permitted to operate, possibly the Bapress
Dowager's Household, or the Peers' school. In such cases, the ac-
counts should be kept by the institutions concerned. In particular,
the accounting of the crowm forests, farms and stock farms, should be
kept in the Special Forestry and Estates Account by the Acoounts
Section (Tokei-ka) of the Imperial Household and Estates Bureau, or
by whatever agency succeeds it.
c. It will probably be most convenient to assign all budgetry
and accounting functions, other than Special Accounts, to the Imperial
2 See Civil Affairs Quide on Preservation of Key Recorda in Japan.
18008 P40 bu

=== Page 122 === (confidence 0.97)
-37-
c. It will probably be most convenient to assign all budgetary
and accounting functions, other than Special Accounts, to the Dmperial
Treasury Bureau, which may be retained possibly in a modified form.
If it is decided to dissolve that Bureau, the Crowm Property Custodian
should make use of its experienced personnel.
d. In general, customary accounting and budgotary procedures
should is retained as far as possibie for convenience of Mù. The
detailed balance sheet and other important documents should be drawm
up in parallel Japanese and English texts.
11. Auditing
Anditing formerly carried on by the Board of Imperial Anditors
should be similarly assigned to a board of anditors, appointed by the
Crowm Property Custodian. This board should consist of carefully
screened Japanese with a knowledge of English and adequate technical
training.

Definition of geographical terms used:
aza - hamlet, subdivision of casa
cho - precinot
fu - urban prefecture
gun - county
ken - rural prefecture
ku - ward
machi - urban towmship, or precinot
mra - rural towmahip
oaza - hamlet, sometimes subdivision of shi
shi - city

Eaclosure to Despatch No. 21l of Jamuary 18, 1946 from the
United States Political Adviser, Tokyo, Japan on the subjeet
"Tramsmitting Copy of Welfare Ministry Ordimance Forbidding

Welfare Ministry Ordinance No. 2,
promulgated 10 Jamuary 1946.
Under Imperial Ordimance No. 542, of 1945, the mployment and
terms of labor for workers are prescribed as follows:
10 January 1946.
Hitoshi Ashida,
Minister of Welfare.
Welfare Ministry Ordinance No. 2
Article l. No proprietor of a factory, plant, or other working
place shall give any discrimimative treatment concerning wages, sala-
ries, working hours, or other terms of labor either for or agaimst
workers in his service because of their nationality, religion or so-
cial status.
Article 2. The local governor or Chief of the Labor Office is
authorized to require a report from persons concermed, if deemed
mecessary for the enforcement of the present Ordinance.
The local govermor or Chief of the Labor Office is authoried to
have an iaspection made by competent officials of the conditions of
labor, books, and other documents, as well as other relevant matters,
by paying a personal visit to the factory, plant, or other working
place, if he deems it necessary for the enforcement of the present
Ordimamce. In this case, the competent officials shall carry with
them the credentials shown on the annexed sheet indicating their of-
ficial status.
Article 3. Any person who has contravened the provisions of
Article 1 shall be liable to a fine not exceeding 3,000 yen.
Article 4. Any person who has failed to submit the report speci-
fied in paragraph 1 of Article 2, made a false entry ia his report, or
refused, impeded, or evaded an inspection by competent officials as
provided for in paragraph 2 of the same Article shall be liable to a
fine not exceeding 500 YEN.
Article 5. In case the representative of a juridical person, or
an agent, employee, or other worker of a juridical person or indivi-
dual has acted in violation of Article 3 or the first clause of the
preceding Article with respect to the business of the said juridical
person or individual, the said juridicel person or individual shall be
liable to the fimes as provided for in the preceding two Articles in
addition to the person at fault.
Supplementary Provisions
This Ordinance shall come iato force as from the day of promulga-
tiom.

Four members of the War Department (Civilian) Labor
1
20
Commission to Japan, including the Chairman, Dr. Eberling,
and Mr. W. H. McPherson met with Messrs. Holland, Ross,
4
Swayzee, Fishburn and Sullivan at 2:00 p.m. on Wednesday,
9
February 6 for a discussion of problems relating to
Japanese labor and the projected WFTU Commission visit to
Japan. After a preliminary talk in Mr. Holland's office
the meeting moved over to Mr. Mulliken's office where the
members of the Commission were briefed in respect to
current labor developments in Japan. State Department
opinion in regard to some of the provisions of Japan's
2
new Trade Union Law and other current labor problems was
explained. We were encouraged to learn that the Commission
has now been authorized to include consideration of labor
legislation matters as well as their three original func-
2
-2046
tions of social security, wage and employment stabilization.
Though the labor movement and trade unionism are still not
specifically listed as matters directly concerning them,
it would seem that they must deal somewhat with problems
FTLED
of this nature incidental to their work on labor legis-
lation.

Two important aids to us may flow from this meeting.
First, the Commission realizes State's interest in this
field, and are informed o our views relating to specific
problems. Second, arrangements have been made so that
we may hope to receive from the Commission a flow of
materials relating to labor developments there.
Mr. Mc Pherson spent most of February 1l in ILH
perusing files of materials dealing with labor develop-
ments in Japan. Copies of ILH memoranda on these
developments were given him for use of the Commission.

OFFICE OF THE UNITED STATES POLITICAL ADVISER
Tokyo, Japan, March 23, 1946
No. 319
SUBJECT:
Suggested Legal Justifications for Labor's Assertion
Of Managerial Powers in Japanese Labor Disputes
4 36
The Hongrable
894.504/3-234€
2
The Secretary of State,
P.3S
Washington.
sfr:
I have the honor to transmit herewith a memorandum prepared
by a member of the staff of this Office, entitled "Suggested Legal
Justifications for Labor's Assertion of Managerial Powers in
Japanese Labor Disputes."
A mumber of recent laber disputes in Japan have been accompanied
by the seisure and exercise of manageriel controls by workers.
While the forms and degrees of control have varied, in most in
stances such actions have been denounced by employers as illegal.
JA
No judicial or police support of employers has yet been noted,
although the Japanese Government on February 1 stated an inten-
tion to prosecute further infringements on "the rights of owmership".
FEC(ERD)
CS/LF
oci
Labor groups and labor sympathizers, especially the Communists,
144
have attacked the February 1 statement of policy on various grounds,
perhaps the most interesting being a defense of the legality of
laborts exercise of managerial powers, which was made by Dr. SU
HIRO Izutaro, a well-known member of the Faculty of Law at Tokyo
Imperial University, in an article appearing in the Mainichi on
February 7 and 8, 1946. The opinions expressed therein (and by
Dr. SUEHIRO personally in a discussion with Mr. Durgin and Mr.
Blakemore of this Office) form the basis of this memorandum.
Admittedly Dr. SUEhIRO's legal theories are controversial,
and it is not the purpose of this memorandum to speculate on
the validity or likelihood of their ultimate acceptance. His
arguments themselves, however, may be regerded as an ingenious
effort to justify a form of trespass, and the publicity afforded
them is evidence both of an intellectual interest in labor prob-
lems and a public concern over matters of legality.
Respectfully yours,
baoowBishop
294 504/2
Max W. Bishop
Foreign Service Officer

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure to despatch No. 319 dated March 23, 1946, from the
Office of the United States Political,Adviser, Tokyo, Japan.
on the subject "Suggested Legal Justifications for Laborts
Assertion of Managerial Powers in Japanese Labor Disputes".
Suggested Legal Justifications of Labor's Assertion
of Managerial Powers in Japanese Labor Disputes
BACKGROUND:
Among the issues involved in recent labor disturbances in
Japan, that of labor's exercise of managerial controls has fre-
quently appeared. Ten of the one hundred and thirty-nine laber
disputes which ocourred in Jamuary, 1946, resulted in labor's
assumption of some form or degree of control over company or
plant management. The most publicised instances have been the
Hitsubishi Coal dispute in Hokkaido, the Yopiuri-Hochi Hewspaper
dispute in Tokyo, and the Keisei Densha (Keisei Mlectric Rail-
road) dispute in Saitama. The Iomiuri-Hochi difficulty arose
in November over the refusal of employees to accept the manage-
ment of editor-owner SHoRIkI Masutaro. After several weeks of
staff operation, an interim agreement was reached whereky the
management was made subject to the approval of empleyees. In
Hokkaido, a worker's committee has operated the Mitsubishi Miuta
coal mine since Jammary. Oontinued participation in management
was not an issue in the Keisei Densha dispute, but those company
directors whose resignations had been requested by workers, on
the ground of "war guilt", were forced out of office in Jammary.
Apparently disturbed by labor's repeated exercise of mana-
gerial controls and seizure of property, the Ministers of Home
Affaire, Commerce and Industry, Justice, and Welfare, on Febru-
ary 1 made reference to these cases in the following joint state-
ment of the polioy of the Japanese Government:
"While the development and activity of labor
unions is to be hoped for, the Government regrets
to see that cases of violence, coercion, and in-
fringement of the rights of ownership (underscoring
added) have of late taken place in labor disputes.
Such practices should never be resorted to by work-
ers conscious of their responsibility for rebuilding
a new Japan. The Government will be obliged to take
decisive measures respecting such unjust and lawless
actions. The Government earnestly desires that work-
men will exercise full caution lest they should commit
such misconduct."
This declaration aroused considerable criticism from laber
sympathisers, mainly on the ground that employers, through their
refusals to increase plant production in the face of current
business uncertainties, wers engaging in "sit down" strikes,
and that temporary exercises of managerial controls by labor
were acts in the national interest. (Bed Flas, February 5 and 9;
Asahi, February 8: Yomiuri. February 3. All of these papers
consistently follow the Communist "party line".)
A a

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.97)
-3
As a result of such protests, the governmentts poliey was re-
considered at a Cabinet meeting on February 8. Mo agreemant ums
reached, and press comment indicated that a deadlock had arisen
over the question of the legality of laborês use of managerial
powers. Members of the cabinet who were inclined to sympathise
with laborts position were reportedly met with the firm insistanes
of government lawyers that such labor controls over management
are definitely illegal. (Tokyo Shimbun, February 14, 1946.) No
government modification of its policy has been announced as of
the date of this memorandum.
On February 7 and 8 a two article series entitled "The Legality
and Limitations of Laborts Exercise of Managerial Controls" appear
ed in the Hainichi. The suthor, SUEHIRO Isutaro, is one of the
best known legal scholars in Japan. He is a professor and former
Dean of the Law Department of Tokyo Imperial University, and at
present is actively engaged in drafting labor legislation for
the Ministry of Welfare. For more than twenty years his juris-
pradential essays and discussions of current legal problems,
usually written with a liberal point of view, have been widely
publicised in both legal and popular periodicals,
The following description of Dr. SUHIROts theories concern-
ing this particular phase of Japanese labor law is submitted after
a discussion with Dr. SUEHIRO and after a study of the Hainighi
articles referred to above. No opinion is offered of the in-
trinsic value of Dr. SUEHIRO's arguments, which he himself feels
may be somewhat too progressive to be accepted by the present
Cabinet. Rather, this description is made for the following
reasons:
(1) The theoretical basis of this approach to labor problems
indicates that the labor movement in Japan has a certain active
intellectual support.
(2) A significant popular concern over matters of legality
may be reflected in the fact that a newspaper of no especial
leftist leanings (Mainichi) allocates front-page space to a
lengthy, legalistic discussion of this nature.
(3) SUmHIRO:s original and ingenious legal defense of a
form of labor conduct which is particularly difficult to fustify
on conventional grounds may be of interest to appropriats agencies
of the Government.
THE VIEWS OF DR. SUBHIRO IZUTARO ON THE LEGALITY AND LIMITATIONS
OF LABOR*S EXERCISE OF MANAGERIAL CONTROLS
Dr. SUmmImO specifically excludes from his consideration
of the legality of labor's assertion of managerial powers those
non-legal fustifications which usually have been presented by
labor sympathisers. As a lawyer, he wishes to deal with legal-
ities alone, not with equities such as social or economic con-
siderations.
The basic concept of Dr. SUEHIRO's argument is that labor
now possesses a "right of dispute" (sosiken), as a result of the
enactment of the new Labor Union Law (Rodo Kumiai ho). This
right

=== Page 155 === (confidence 0.98)
-3
right also will be recognised in the Labor Disputes Arbitration
Law (Rodo Sogi Choteiho), the preliminary draft of which is now
being revised in the special Labor Legislation Committee appeinted
by Prime Minister SHIDEHARA.
Artiele 1 of the Labor Union Law provides:
"The object of the present ast is to raise the
status of workers and thereby contribute to economic
development, through the guarantee of the right of
organisation (Danketsuken) and the protection and
promotion of the right of collective bargaining (Dan-
tai Kooho ken).
"The provisions of Article 35 of the Criminal
Code shall apply to collective bargaining and other
proper (seito) acts (underscoring added) of labor
unions which are performed for the attainment of the
objective of the preceding paragraph."
(NOTE: Article 35 of the Criminal Code provides:
"Acts which are performed in accordance with law
or proper (seito) business shall not be punishable.")
In recognizing collective bargaining, the above article makes
it perfectly clear, says Dr. SUkHIRO, that those legal objections
which formerly were made to the organisation of labor are no longer
valid. Heretofore the contract of employment between each laborer
and his emphoyer was regarded as giving rise to certain rights
and dutiss which would be illegally disturbed through collective
bargaining. Laborers, acting through their unions, now are ex-
pected to bargain collectively. Both labor unions and employers
are given equal "rights of dispute", and in the exercise of
these rights, as in any true bargaining transaction, each party
is fres to use arguments, inducements, and pressures in seeking
to prevail. After a free process of bargaining has occurred.
presumably agreements will be reached and contracts will result.
In past labor struggles, both employers and laborers have
applied a variety of bargaining pressures. Employere have re-
sorted to lockouts, blacklists, have refused to deal with unions.
and have invoked police and judicial assistance. Workers, in
turn, have organized strikes, slow-downs, demonstrations, boy-
cotts, have picketed and committed acts of violence, eto. Re-
contly labor has developed this new method of seizing managerial
controls.
Some of the above-mentioned methods (or weapons of dispute)
are illegal, while others are within the law. Dr. sUmHIRO be-
lieves that labor use of violenoe, blackmail, coercion, and
damage of plant property are clearly and unquestionably illegal.
Acts such as these are crimes under the Criminal Code and are
subject to punishment. The prosecution of individuals committing
such crimes is just and proper. However, such crimes have not
occurred in recent labor disputes involving the exercise of
managerial powers. In implying that they have (see ministerial
statement of February 1), Dr. SUZHIRO claims that the government
is deliberately obscuring issues.
The teot

=== Page 156 === (confidence 0.98)
The test of legality of a weapon of labor used in collee-
tive bargaining is stated in Paragraph 2 of Artiole 1 of the
Labor Union Law (quoted above). Acts which are "proper" mmst
be accepted as logal. This standard of legality is vague, end
in csrtain situations there will be differences of opinion. It
would seem evident that acts which constitute crimes are im-
proper. On the other hand, those which are specifically pro-
vided for in labor laws, certainly must be in the "proper"
category. Labor's right to strike, and its right to negotiate
(Article 10 of Labor Union Law) are examples of "proper. hence
legitimate acts.
How does a temporary assertion of managerial controls by
labor meet the above standard of legality? Dr. SUmhIRO sees
at least two possible arguments in favor of the acceptance
of such an action as proper, and thus legal. The most cogent
is an analogy. Under present law, labor is given the right to
strike-- powerful weapon which can work great damage upon
employers. Yet, damaging though this may be, it is perfectly
"proper". Therefore, is it not logical to regard as equally
acceptable a much weaker weapon (from the standpoint of labor)
-one which brings much less pressure to bear upon the employer.
namely the temporary assumption of managerial controls?
Another possible support of the legality of labors use of
managerial powers might be found in the provisions of Articles
Nos. 691-702 of the Civil Code, which concern "business manage
mont"(Jim Kanri).
(MOTE: "Business management" is a relationship kmown to Roman
Law as Negotiorium Gestio. It arises when one person, vithout
previous authority, and without a legal duty so to do, mamages
the affairs of another person. A common illustration is the
assumption of control over the property of a friend during his
absence. Upon the creation of the relationship, the person
assuming managerial powers is charged with various duties, such
as using good care, "acting in accordance with the wishes of
the principal if he kmows or is able to conjecture them" and
continuing his management until such time as the principal is
in a position to act for himself.
In such a situation, the "consent" of the principal to
this safeguard of his interests is presumed at law. To apply
this theory of business management to labor's exercise of
managerial powers in cases where employers have vigorously and
specifically protested labor controls would appear to be im-
proper and unwarranted.)
Both of these justifications of the "propriety" of labor's
managerial acts can be countered by arguments based on the con-
ventional theories of property rights. The accepted concept is
that owmership confers unlimited powers of management, and that
infringements of non-owners, such as laborers, is illegal.
Thus, it appears that acts which could be justified as
proper in one sphere of law might be regarded as improper upon
the application of the concepts of another. How should the
conflict in interpretations be settled?
Dro

=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.98)
5
Dr. sUkhIno suggests that the existing property conoepts.
rather than the new laber legislation, are unrealistic and
should be modified. As a practical matter, he says, aren't
there three factors in modern business-capital, labor, and
management! Although the present corporation law may still re»
gard as supreme the control of owners over management, actually
labor unions (backed by the Labor Union Law) are now bargaiming
with owners on an equal footing. Both may legitimately mmke
demands concerning the conduct of business. In a going con-
cern, therefore, the factor of management is distinct from
ownership. It lies somewhere in between ownership and laber
and is responsive to pressures from both.
fore
Therd, in event of a conflict of standards of propriety.
those indicated in the new labor legislation ought to prevail
over those of older fields of law, which are based on outmoded
concepts of business operations. Dr. Suehiro concludes that
temporary acts of management by workers, if unaccompanied by
conduct otherwise of a criminal nature, should be regarded as
lawful and should not be subject to punishment.
Obviously the distinction between temporary and permanent
assertions of managerial control is vital in Dr. SUBHIROºs
analysis. While temporary controls, coming as an incident in
a labor dispute and used by labor simply as another weapon,
may be justified, Dr. SUEHIRO admits that any permanent parti-
cipation of labor in management must be with the consent of
owmers. In certain cases the dividing line between permanent
and temporary may be hard to draw, although usually it is clear.
Whenever labor finds it necessary to engage in financial trans-
actions on behalf of the plant or company or to contract for
services or supplies, it is getting into the realm of permanent
management. As a practical matter, temporary management usually
would consist of continuing plant operations with existing
stocks of raw materials.
Labor aspirations for a share in management are perfectly
legitimate, but any enduring rights of this nature must come
from agreements with employers. Already in one company, Hitachi.
owners have agreed to allow a union a certain voice in managerial
matters. However, since under existing corporation law manage
ment is made an exclusive right of ownership, this participation
of unions in management may be difficult to arrange. A revision
of corporation law to facilitate labor contracts may prove de-
sirable.

Appendix
The Legality and the Limitations of Labor's
Exercise of Management Controls
by SUEHIRO Isutaro
(Mainichi Shimbun, February 7, 8. 1946)
As much discussion as has been aroused by the recent actions
of labor in asserting controls over the management of businesses,
a sound analysis of the legal position of these acts has not yet
appeared. The recent declaration of four Government ministers
(to the effect that illegal acts of strikers would be punished)
not only obscured the issues, but fostered public hostility to
labor through its misrepresentations. Actually the government's
denunciation of violence, coercion and the damage to property
was a "red herring". Of course such actions are illegal. They
were not present at all in the recent labor disputes which led
to laborss asmumption of controls over management.
At the outset, a clear distinction must be made between
laborts right of dispute (Sogiken) and the various means and
methods used by labor in the implementation of that right. A
further difference mmst be recognised between acts of control
over the management of a plant or industry, which result imed-
dentally or temporarily from labor's exercise of its right of
dispute, and the right of participation in management which labor
hopes (ultimately) to gain through collective bargaining. Failure
to grasp these distinctions has lead to many unfortunate mis-
understandings between labor and owners.
Sometimes the opinion is voiced that labor has no right of
dispute at all. This opinion applies to labor disputes the prin-
ciples of private law which pertain to contracts between indivi-
duals. A contract of employment--so the argument goes-imposed
upon the laborer (as a party to the contract), certain obliga-
tions. Any evasion of these duties, through collective bargain-
ing, is illegal.
If such a concept of law were applied, labor again would
be completely subject to the arbitrary and unilateral acts of
employers. But it is perfectly clear that the analogy to private
contracts is bad. Both the Labor Dispute's Negotiation Law
(Rodo Sogi Choteiho) and the Labor Union Law (Bodo Kumiai ho)
are predicated on the right of labor to dispute. With the de-
velopment of labor unions, it is safe to predict that decisions
in the form of labor contracts, based on collective bargaining
(Shudanteki Torihiki) will replace the unilaterial decisions of
employers. In collective bargaining naturally points of view
vary, and disputes arise. In capitalist countries, no more is
done than to put pressure on both parties to negotiate and
settle disputes which are harmful to the public interest. The
settlement must be by the free will of both sides. Therefore,
it is clearly

=== Page 159 === (confidence 0.98)
2
it is clearly erreneous to regard the right of dispute had by a
workman as being contrary to his duties under a labor agreement.
Everyone must see this. It logically followe that empleyers
also have rights of dispute: these mmst be recoghised.
In any dispute, each side seeks to persuade or force the
other to yield. Both laborers and employers have used a variety
of methods in the past, and new techniques can be expected. 
ployers have used the weapons of lockout, refusal to deal with
organizations, blacklist, police power, and judicial power.
Laborers have used general strikes (Domei Hisyo),amlowdoums
(Taigyo), demonstrations, boycott, picketing, acts of violence,
stc. The latest method is exercise of control over the manage
mont of plants.
It is difficult to lay down any single rule which indicates
which acts or methods are legal and which are illegal. Artiole 1,
Paragraph 2 of the Labor Union Law, however, is an important in-
dicator. It provides that labor unions may make "proper acts",
in accord with the spirit of Paragraph 1 (the basic statement
of objective of the law). Such "proper acts" are not subject
to punishment.
Opinions naturally will vary as to the propriety of acts.
Certain categories--such as acts of violence, property theft.
destruction, and intimidation  are clearly illegal and should be
punished as crimes. On the other hand, a general strike which
interferes with the operation of a factory and likewise damages
the owner, is not a crime. Nor is a demand on the part of labor
representatives to confer with company officials, which is re-
cognized under article 10 of the labor union law as a valid
exercise of the power of negotiation. There is no such crime
as that of "forcing an interview".
The issue of the legality or illegality of labor's exer-
cise of managerial powers is completely different from that of
the legality or illegality of acts which are incidental to the
exercise of managerial controls. Examples of illegal means of
obtaining management control would be the locking out of company
officials, or the disposition of company property. Quite right-
ly, acts of this nature are crimes, and could be prosecuted as
false arrest, embezzlement, or theft.
However, such acts did not occur in the recent Yomiuri
Press and Keisei Electric Railroad disputes. Throughout the
difficulty, good order was preserved and advice was taken from
managers as to things which had to be done. No acts occurred
which can be regarded as illegal. In the Yomiuri incident. the
leader of the employees was given notice of dismissal, but con-
tinued to come to work. The president of the company then sought
to bring charges of trespass under Article 130 of the Criminal
Code which penalizes an act of unlawful entrance and refusal to
leave.
This act of an employer in attempting to prevent labor from
organising was in direct violation of the spirit of Paragraph 2
of Article 1 of the Labor Law. Therefore, the employee's ignoring
of the attempt at dismissal was quite proper.
In contrast

=== Page 160 === (confidence 0.99)
3
In contrast to this rolatively simple solution of the legal-
ity of acts committed incidentally in the assertion of managerial
powers, the legality of the control over management itself is
more difficult. Legal scholars may disagres, but there are two
or three theories which can be advanced to uphold the legality.
Non-legal arguments, such as are now heard to the effect that
employers are sabotaging the national effort through inaction
and that national interests require positive steps to restore
prodgction, are not considered here.
The most cogent argument in support of the legality of
labor's control over management is this. Harmful though a strike
is to national and public interests, it nevertheless is a legal-
ly recognized weapon of labor. Assertion of managerial powers
are far less injurious than strikes, and in public utilities
are preferable. If the stronger solution is legal, the lesser
should be acknowledged as legal also.
Another theory is that labor's assertion of management
powers during a dispute is fustified as an assumption of business
custody under the provision of Article 694 of the Civil Code.
Both of these theories run headlong into conventional and
deep-seated concepts of property rights, and definition of in-
fringement. The conventional view is that the owner of pmoperty
has unqualified powers of management, which are not affected by
labor disputes. Therefore, labor's exercise of managerial con-
trols constitutes an infringement. Labor must not lay a finger
on property without the consent of the owner.
Modern scholars are tending to take a different view of the
right of management, feeling that the old conceptions of property
are not in trend with the times. As a practical matter, the
capitalist now merely contributes ownership to the business:
this is combined with labor in general, and with management to
result in operations. Although commercial law still recognizes
the supreme power of a stockholder's assembly over management,
labor law now casts doubt on this authority. Under the new
labor law, capital and labor ere on an •qual footing. Both
have a right to engage in disputes. Management is caught be-
tween two fires.
Labor now uses the temporary control over management as a
method of prevailing in disputes. On the basis of the labor
law, the owmers: assertion of claims of property infringement,
and their appeals to government authorities are most inequitable.
Labor is doing no more than making use of an additional weapon
in pressing its legitimate right of dispute and as such, the
acts are "proper" within the meaning of Paragraph 2 of Article
1 of the Labor Union Law. They are covered by Article 35 of the
Criminal Code (which forbids punishaent for lawful acts).
The government would make a grave mistake in applying for-
mer concepts of private law to the present situations. But also
labor must use great care not to use improper measures in press-
ing claims.
▲ permanent

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.95)
4
A permanent participation of labor in management, er a
permanent exercise of rights of management is quite another
matter. The scope of labor's rights of participatien in
management, even if secured through labor contract, is excsed-
ingly ciroumscribed under existing law. Managerial powers
stem from stockholder's assemblies, and to provide for laber
participation a revision of Company law would be necessary.

CHAPTER I. GENERAL PRINCIPLES
Art. 1. The aim of this Law shall be, in conjunction with the Trade Union
Law, to promote a fair adjustment of labor relations and to prevent or settle
labor disputes end thereby to contribute to the maintenence of industrial peace
and to economic development.
Art. 2. The parties concerned with labor relations shall make special
endeavors mutually to promote proper and feir lahor relations, and fix by Trade
Agreement metters relating to the establishment as well as menegement of regular
agencies to adjust differences constently, end in the event thet lsbor disputes
occur, to endeavor to settle them autonomously in ell sincerity.
Art. 3. The government shell assist the parties concerned with 1.bor re-
lations to find a settlement of the differences of their claims concerning labor
relations in order thereby to prevent to the utmost the occurrence of the acts
of dispute.
Art. 4. Nothing in this law shall be construed either to prevent the
parties from determining for themselves their 1shor relations or from adjusting
the differences of their cleims concerning lebor relntions by direct negotia-
tions or collective bergeining or to relieve the parties concerned with lebor
relations of their responsibility for making such endeevors.
Art. 5. In effecting any edjustment under this present law, the parties
and the Labor Reletions Committee end other organs concerned should as fer es
possible utilize evory appropriate convenience to expedite the disposal of the
cse.
Art. 6. In this lew, labor dispute shell meen & disegreement of claims
arising between the perties concerned with labor reletions regarding lebor re-

or conditions with the denger of their occurrence.

enterprise, performed by the perties concerned with labor relations with the
object of ettrining their respective cleims.

Janan's Labor Hovement
Recent magazine writing on the subfect of labor covers a
wide range -- from tracts on the underlying económic causes for
labor unrest, through studies of the unions and the possibility
of their unification, to interpretations of general labor currents
and the outlook for the future. Hany of the labor writers appear
to be conscious of the obligations of the unions and their members;
others view the labor movement variously as a means of social,
economic, or nolitical struggle to improve the lot of the workers
alone. Few extrame oninions are noted and many writers, backing
their stands with documentation, show evidence of considerable study
of the problems. It is often stressed, too, that union actions
should reflect the will of the majority of their members, rather
than that of the leaders alone, and considerable concern is shown
for the education of workers in order that they may be able to
understand and direct their own movements.
Recent Labor Develonments
Most of the writers in this field are advocates of a strong
labor movement; therefore, whether they are writing to urge labor
to greater activity or to clide union members for their faults,
their point of view is usually prolabor. Kihachiro Kimura is
typical in his SUUKAN ASAHI summary of labor developments and the
outlook for the future. Kimura commences by quoting an ASAHI edi-
torial which says that salaried workers and farmers suffer most
from inflation. Consequently, according to the editorial, "the
problem of inflation imnlies class strife. Strife for higher wages
is not sufficient, and workers and farmers must embark on a poli-
tical movement in order to check the causes of inflation." Those
who are favored by inflation naturally will do nothing to halt
rising prices, Kimura asserts - hence, "it is un to the workers and
farmers to launch the industrial recovery movement. Accordingly,
I believe that the industrial reconstruction movement must arise
sponteneously among the labor groups."
larly labor activity featured such aims as establishing the
rights of workers and receiving nrotection against discharge. Now,
declares Kimura, labor's goals have been extended to embrace
positive action aiming at solution of the production crisis and the
reconstruction of industry. The J.F.L. therefore inaugurated the
Fconomic Reconstruction Conference, "favoring a program of indust-.
rial recovery conferences along the lines of the management
conference, and expressed the view that industrial recovery would be
difficult without the cooneration of the canitalists." Meanwhile,
the N.C.I.O., sponsoring the Industrial Recovery Conference,
"maintained vigorously that  movement by the labor groups should
be held concurrently with the canitalist-controlled management
conference." (Before this article was published, the N.C.I.O. had
already made its decision to participate in the jRC; however, the
IRC was not abandoned and is still active.)

=== Page 188 === (confidence 0.97)
The writer charges that many purgees, continuing their aoti
ties under cover, are holding up Janan's democratization, and are
behind the Government's nresent inflationary policy. "Under this
circumstance, a unified labor front of the J.F.L. and N.C.I.O. will
be hard to realize and cooperation between labor and capital will
be the more hopeless." Replecing the present Government is the
only solution to the country's economic problem, Kimura says, and
"when the time is ripe for the establishment of democratic govern-
ment, unity between the J.F.L. and the N.C.I.O. will be achieved
quickly and & patriotic industrial recovery movement will be launch-
ed throughout the countrw."

Methods of Achievement of Labor Goals
4 simple, explonatory article on the labor unions, presented in
question and answer form by Keizo Fujibayashi, anpears in FUJIN
GiHO. The key objectives of the lsbor unions are described as the
establishment of job nd economic security, and improvement of
working conditions. Management by the wili of the individual
members is mentioned as a democratic feature of the unions, which
aim to close the gap between capitalists and workers by means of
labor participation in management.

Fujibayashi also considers labor problems in two other
megazine articles. In BUNGrI SINJU ho meintains that labor
tions are still rolativoly unstcbilized bccause management hen
relatively uninterested in coming to terms. (This article pree

affairs. "One great change is that disputes are becoming fair.
Sccondly, disputes take the form of strikes, but not of production
management." However, Fujibayeshi believes that labor unions in
Janan are for fron maturcd, and points out treir recklessness in

lost during the disnute. Counting on such settlements, they have

settloments for back pay re injurious to the healthful growth
of the labor movement; for, after all, it is the indulgonce of
management which thus sunports the unions. at, the writer asks,
would hanpen to the unions should mnagement fofuse this back
pay for lost tine? Labor should become better organized and more
thoughtful of tre future, cautions Fujibayashi, before projecting
largo-scale offensives upon so unstabic  foundation.

I. new attitude toward labor lavs must bo adonted, according to
Takenobu Kawashima, Tokyo Imperial University nrofessor of law, in

a changing of the old order, in the direction of soqial

laws which fit snugly into the existing civil legal structui
says, but a new concept, based on sociological study of moder

Education of Unionists
Both critics and labor spokesmen agree that education of the
working classes is a necessary condition to a healthy labor move
ment. One of the critics, Dr. Kazuo Okochi of Tokyo Imperial Uni-
versity, mentions the need for labor education in NIPPON DOKUSHO
SHIMBUN. Because "union members have no knowledge of their position
they are not well informed concerning the social system and, above
all, they lack any sense of labor ethics. Thus we seriously doubt
whether they can play an important part in the program of demoerati-
zation; aiso we draw the conclusion that the labor union has neither
the desire nor the capacity to participate constructively in joint
management." Hidezo Kondo, MANGA cartoonist, charges that most
strikers are unaware of the significance of what they are doing,
although he is "of the opinion that under the existing economic
structure strike action is unavoidable in order to secure a decent
living for the masses." But Kondo is irritated by the careless.
attitude of labor leaders. "If the chairmen of the committees or
any other leaders believe that their principles and policies are
justly founded in humanity, why do they not take the trouble to ex-
plain this? Militant leaders, I will cease to complain if you be-
lieve that the time for enlightenment has passed. But I, who am
confident that a bloodless revolution must have its moving spirit
in the masses and in their complete education, will have to turn
aside from the red flags which represent a forced and unnatural
movement."
The need for education is illuminated in a FUJIN NO ToMO ar-
ticle by Tokuko Yamemuro about the girls who do clerical and clean-
ing work at Ueno Station in Tokyo. The long hours and poor working
conditions (the cleaning girls get up at four-thirty in the morning
and get to bed at midnight, the story relates) are described graph-
ically. "e are always tired," one of the girls is quoted as say
ing, "and so we cannot appreciate new ideas. The story of the labor
unions is also hard to understand. Though I was persuaded to join
a demonstration, I went home because I was too sleepy." The writer
then advises: "It is to be desired that they be given education and
encouragement, which will afford them interest in their work, be-
cause they now work merely from the habits acquired in wartime."
A KAI20 piece by Fukuo Otomo studies the Japanese laborer cla:
asserting that the whole world has known of their long hours, low
wages and feudal working conditions. "It is said that Japanese cap
italism, strangling all freedom, created wage-labor of the lowest
type, deep-rooted in the primitive agricultural economy. An inde-
pendent and self-disciplined class has not been formed, and persons
with initiative, high social standards and labor fervor have never
developed. * * * It is evident that labor unions, organized from
those workers, have no fervor,and no ability to participate posi-
tively in management. Thus it becomes necessary to re-educate
workers from this feudal group to a modern productive class." Ja-
pan did have a few progressives in prewar times who fought against
the most difficult conditions. This fact, plus "the strength of
the labor movement suddenly given expression after the defeat, and
the steady strides of labor are proving to us that the immaturity
of our labor class is not immutahle -- and Japanese laborers are
acquiring increased class consciousness through their struggles.

=== Page 195 === (confidence 0.99)
Trend3. Warnings, and Hopes
In an article deploring the condition of his country, Hitoshi
Yamakawa, writing for TAIHrI, finds grounds for hope in the labor
movement. (It is to be noted that Yamakawa is a prominent Social-
ist and may be considered to be speaking at least partially for the
J.F.L.) "It is noticeable that the salaried business class is
holding the reins in the prevailing labor movement. * * * The
leaders are generally intellectuals with university degrees. They
are said to be the most radical elements in the labor movement 
if they were cold to it, such rapid development could not have been
expected. * * * It is a significant social phenomenon that engineers
and business men have made up their minds to share the fortune of
the workers. Labor unions which include the staff members of a
company naturally will try for cooperation rather than outright
strife."
An FKONOMISUTO staff article lauds the N.C.I.O. participation
in the Fconomic Reconstruction Conference, declaring that this shows
the desire of labor and capital to join in a cooperative effort.
The article also condemns the Yoshida Government for anti-labor
policies: "The crisis must be surmounted at cost to either the
capitalists or the mass of workers. Is the Yoshida Government --
which asserted in a New Year's statement that the labor movement is
a dangerous action by lawless people -- confident of surmounting
the crisis? * * * Vithout proper recognition of the labor unions
there can be no industrial rehabilitation. Yoshida Government,
wake up or resign! It is impossible to surmount the present crisis
unless Government, labor and capital cooperate frankly."
The history of general strikes is traced in KAIZO by Iwao
Ayusawa, who note: that both the people and the governments of the
United States and Ingland oppose such moves. "In the future," he
writes, "the general strike will not be practiced and will not
need to be practiced." Its previous results have not been good, he
says, and as social policies come to reflect the popular will more
and more, its causes will be removed. Only if they are oppressed
continuously by undemocratic power, must the Japanese laborers rise.
Such necessity is removed if "we enforce the most thoroughly
democratic labor policy."
Jananese labor is not striving for the actual ideal goals set
up for it in the new Constitution, writes Kazuo Okochi in HIKARI.
Instead of seeking to realize the rights of "a healthy and cultural
minimum standard of living," they are simply pressing demands for
immediate benefits. At the same time, the State should "strive for
the improvement and advancement of social welfare, social security,
and nublic health." Labor is showing no concern with establishing
what he calls "the worthwhile life," says this author, and is not
sincere in its request to be allowed to take a responsible part in
management.
Constructive Advice
One of the most objective of all the labor essays is an
editorial in the labor review RODO HYORON. The author comments
on the surprising progress made by Jananese unions up through the
beginning of this year. His figures show the greatest number of
unions in prewar years to have been 993 (in 1935) and the largest
membership 420,589 (in 1936). In 1941 there were 11 unions with
895 members. Now there are more than 15,000 unions and 4,000,000
members. The writer warns, however, that this development is not
something which has been "won" entirely by the laborers them-
selves. "Our labor union movement has made a rapid outward
development, under the guidance of the Allied forces, and although

=== Page 196 === (confidence 0.95)
1ts full intcrnal development lcaves much to be demir
gained unexpcctodly succossful results in its strife.
"there can be no more dangcrous way of thinking than to be
intoxicated with the successful accomplishments- without rmr
ing upon thoir real causes - and on this basis to take an o
optimistic view of the future. There are many inconsistondiem
and irrationalitics in the growing labor union movoment, and
only by incossant efforts to sottle end overcomo them can we
achieve a real development of the lebor movemont."

TITLE
Some Aspects of Labor Organization in the
Press and Redio in Postwar Japan.
SUBJECT
The development of labor organizations in
the press and radio since the surrender,
with reference to their reletionship to
the growth of a free and responsible
press in Japan.
PREPARED BY
Infornation Media Research
DATES OF STUDY
28 Apri1 1947 to 3 July 1947.
PURPOSE On BACKGI:OUND
To gather and record materials on the subject, in or-
der to provide information on the background and present
status of press and radio employees' unions for the bene-
fit of personnel interested in this aspect of the develop-
ment of a free and responsible press in postwar Japan.
SCOPE
Limited primarily to labor-management relations in a
selected number of enterprises. These enterprises include
newspapers that form a .cross-section of the Japanese press
--large and small, metropolitan and provincial. They ac-
count for more than half of Japan's entire daily circula-
tion--9,878,700 out of a total of approximately 17,705,000.
Also included are the monopoly Broadcesting Corporation of
Japan that reeches the populace through 5,704,256 radio re-
ceivers (as of 1 April i947); and the largest news agency,
Kyodo, that serves virtually all of Jepan's daily news-
papers.

EVALUATION OF DATA
Tris report consists in large pert of factual materi-

Sources are indiceted in footnotes. A conscientious effort
was mede to gather as much information on this extensive
subject from as many sources as time and facilities pormit-

SUMMARY
During the fall of 1945, press unions were orgenized
rapidly and on a wide scale. In a number of instances, the
organized pressure of employees forced reorganizctions
upon the menagements and geve the newly formed unions an
unprecedented degree of participation in adninistration.

INTRODUCTION
3
During 1946, some of these unions or their leaders ex-
tended their activities to the point of teking over basic
manegement functions, such as determination of editorial
policy.
The climax in this process ceme in October 1946,
with the fsilure of a generel press and radio strike called
by the All Japan News end Radio Workers' Union. Techni-
cally, the strike was designed to protect the job rights
of union members who had been discharged on the ground of
interference in the editorial prerogatives of management.
Actually, the strike was part of & politicel offensive
launched by leftist labor leaders.
The chief trends noticeable since then are two: pro-
gress toward lebor-menagement modi vivendi in various en-
terprises; rejection of extremist leadership and policies
by the unions. There is little evidence thet unions at
present actively are exercising improper influence, es-
pecially any that works directly against the development
of a free and responsible press. In the opinion of some
responsible figures of the Japanese press, however, this
quiescence on the part of redicel leftist elements within
press unions is only & temporary and politic withdrawal.1
