THE CONCEPT OF A FTEE AND FESPO E IBLE PRESS
(ne of the fundementel problems in guiding Jepen to-
werd a denocr:tic society is the development of a free and
responsible prcss. Such a press is essential to the cre-
ation of en informed public opinion which, in turn, is es-
sential to the emergencc and perpetnation of a sovereign
populer euthority.
The concept of e free and responsible pross hes de-
veloped out of the experience of frec societies in foster-
ing end preserving their systems of populer governme nt.
.Their experience proves thet the press must have freedom
of infornation in order to obtain legitimete news. It
must heve freedom of expression in order to report and in-
terpret the news.
At the seme time, the press must be rosponsible in
the use of its freedons. The needs of a free society re-
quire thet reporting be as eccurate, objective, complete,
and balanced es humenly possible. They require that in-
terpretation be clearly distinguished from reporting, and
that it be directed to constructive purposes for the food
of a free society.
These responsibilities inevitably are reposed in the
owners of the press and in the managers selected by then.
It is they who are held account:l le, by the free people
they serve, for the responsible use of their freeccms. It
follows, therefore, thet the owners and managers of the
press above all are responsitle to resist pressures from
individuals or groups that seek to monopolize it for their
own benefit.
In modern society, the over-ell responsililities of
the menegers of the press ere multiplied by two primary
factors. The first is that much of the press hes becee
F E E T: IT E D
-4-

THE PROBLEM
5
big business. Only a few people or groups can mobilize
the cepital and facilities that a newspaper of large cir-
culation requires. The second 1s that the citizen needs
as much information and guidance as possible in order to
act intelligently on the complex issues of modern society.
APPLICATION OF THE CONCEPT IN JAPAN
These considerations apply with particuler force in
present-day Japan, where the press hes a vital role to
play in the reversel of the authoritarian tradition, but
is limited by inadequete facilities and an acute paper
shortage.2
At best, the concept of a free and responsible press
enjoyed only a limited reelization in Japen prior to
World War II. During the war, any'semblance of a free
and responsible press vanished in the face of officiel
controls such as censorship, newsprint rationing, amal-
gemation of competing journels, positive direction of
editorial policy, end subsidy of the monopolistic Domei
News Agency.3 The Broadcasting Corporation of Jepan (BCJ)

to two others, and the fifth is printed in Osaka.
This problem is not ecute, however, because of the

presented no problem of control, being a Government-char-
tered monopoly4 under direct official supervision. The
major distortion of the concept, therefore, was the result
of totalitarian officiel influence and direction. The
fact that the press served official purposes during the
war undoubtedly accounts in considerable degree for the
unanimity with which the Japenese supported the wer, end
for the lack of influence on the pert of those few who did
not. Against this beckdrop, the Occupation began.
One of the first concerns of the Supreme Commender for
the Allied Powers (SCAP) was to lay the foundetions for a
free and responsible press. The principel directives to
this end included the Press Code of 19 September 1945, that
set forth standards of prectice for a free press; a direc-
tive of 24 September 1945 thet ordered the Japanese Govern-
ment to disassociate itself completely from control or in-
fluence over the press; end e directive of 27 September
1945 thet specified further steps toward freedom of the
press and of speech.5
Since the early days of the Occupetion, a new threat
to the concept of e free and responsible press hes arisen,
not from the Japanese Government, but from within the
ranks of newspeper and radio employees. The threet has
been exercised through the instrumentality of employee
unions end expressed in influence on editorial policy. In
some ceses, the threet undoubtedly has been the result of
misconception of the freedom of the press on the pert of
employees. In others, it undoubtedly has been the result
of deliberate stretegy to gain control of the media of in-
formetion.
At any rete, the threat hes brought criticism upon
the employees' unions that formed the channel of influence.
This study proposes to survey some aspects of the postwar
rise of labor organization in the press and recent trends
of the unions; and to set forth some observations ebout
present union organizetion and activities.

Chapter 2.
POSTWAR RISE OF LABOR ORGANIZATION
The unionization of workers in newspaper companies
and the BCJ, as well as in other enterprises, has been
one of the remerkable developments in post-surrender
Japan. It is the direct sequence of besic Occupation
policy to encourage "the development of organizations in
labor, industry and agriculture, organized on a demo-
cratic basis."6 The SCAP "civil liberties" directive of
4 October 1945 instructed the Japanese Government to re-
move restrictions on individual liberties, including the
right of free assembly. On 11 October 1945, the Supreme
Commander told the Prime Minister that the "encourage-
ment of the unionizetion of labor" was one of the reforms
the Japanese Government was expected to institute in
order that labor "mey be clothed with such dignity as
will permit it en influential voice in safeguerding the
working man from exploitation and raising his living stand-
ard to a higher level..."7
"DEMOCRATIZATION" DURING 1945
The organization and self-assertion of employeos was
particularly early and spectacular in the press. In
practice, however, the movement first was directed more
toward what its spokesman termed the "democratization" of
the Japanese press than toward specific objectives, such
as higher wages and better working conditions. The "demo-
cratizetion" movement was most clearly manifested in
Japan's three largest and most influential newspapers
--the Asahi, Mainichi, and Yomiuri. A number of provin-
cial papers followed suit, but in their cases the changes

The Asahi's internal reorganization began in mid-
October 1945, when President Nagataka MURAYAMA brought out
a plan to change leading editorial personnel.9 Six men
affected by the proposed change voiced their objections and
served him with a demand for his resignation. In addition,
the resignations of the entire board of directors and of
Seiichi UENO, its chairman, were requested. Finelly, the
employees wanted one of the editorial staff to be named
representative director with authority to reorpanize the
company.10 The announced purpose of these demands ws: to
"clarify" war responsibility and to "democratize" the com-
pany. A meeting of the employees on 19 October endorsed
the demands and in addition proposed the establishment of
an employees' union.
MURAYAMA and UENO were and are majority stockholders
in the Asahi. When MURAYAMA refused to resign, a movemont
was initiated among the employees to secure the recuested
resignations by direct action. The six men to be replcced
stuck to their posts; handbills denouncing MURAYAMA were
published; and ultimetums were served on him. According
to a statement by MURAYAMA,11 the focus of the movement was
in the editorial department, but "leftist elements among
the employees" seized the opportunity to develop a "radical
labor union movement."

Under such pressure, IURAYAMA agreed to resign in

On 3 November 1945, the employees of the Tokyo
office of the Asahi organized themselves formally into
a union. The origins of the union lay in the beginnings
of the dispute in October, when a proclamation appeared
in the name of the entire editorial staff. "...The only
way to meet the responsibility for our newly acquired
freedom of speech is to place the newspaper in tre hands
of the staff which makes the paper," the proclamation
read in part. When the union was formed, it announced
that employee participetion in management, as well as
better wages and working conditions, were its objectives.
In Merch 1946, a "management committee" was instituted
in the Asahi, with employee representatives designated
by the union.
Early in 1946, director NOMURA requested recommenda-
tions from the Asahi staff for nominations for a new
board of directors. Each section of the newspaper selec-
ted delegates to a nominating committee. The committee
passed up to NOMURA a slate of names from among the em-
ployees of the paper--six for directors and one for
accountant-auditor. These names NOMURA recommended to a
shareholders' meeting in April 1946, where they were ac-
cepted by acclamation. At the same time, the shareholders
voted to designate MUfAYAMA and UENO as owners (shashu)
without either rights of participation in management or
responsibility for the conduct of the paper.13

Internal reorganization of the Mainichi was a simpler
process,14 Late in August 1945, some of the leading exec-
utives of the paper resigned from their posts voluntarily.
Subsequent change in the management was facilitated by the
fact that the bulk of the shares of the paper were owned
by the employees. After a mass meeting of employees on
31 October, the directors resigned, and new ones were se-
lected by an employees' committee from the employees'
ranks. Mr. Itsuo KANDA became "representative director"
as well as chief of the editorial bureau. The new board
of 1l directors included eight bureau chiefs and three
auditors. The Mainichi Employees' Union was founded in
Tokyo on 22 November 1945, and in the Csaka and Moji offices
in December 1945. Management councils including representa-
tives of the union and of management were formed in each of
the offices to enable the employees to have a voice in any
matters of concern to them in the publication of the paper.
Yomiuri's internal upheaval began on 19 October 1945,
when staff members led by Tomin SUZUKJ, the chief edi-
torial writer, proposed to organize a union, "democratize"
the company, and improve working conditions.15 On 23 Octo-
ber, a mass meeting of 1,000 employees demanded that the
president and majority stockholder, Matsutaro SHOfIKI, and
other leaders of tre Yomiuri resign; and that editorial
control be turned over to an cmployees' committee. Their
action was motivated by the fact that SHORIKI had followed
since the surrender a policy of suppressing news or edi-

When SHORIKI refused the employees' demands, they
seized control of the newspaper, ejected SHORIKI, and
turned the columns of the Yomiuri against its legal owner.
On 3 December 1945, SPORIKI was designated for ar-
rest as a war crimes suspect. On 12 December, just prior
to his confinement to prison, an agreement with the union
was reached through the good offices of an interim media-
tion committee. The chief provisions of the agreement
were the designation of Tsunego BlBh, a recognized liberal,
as new president of the company; and the organization of
a menagement council. This council, consisting of repre-
sentatives of the union and of the management, was to "be
consulted on important matters related to editing and
business."
The net result of the democratization movement of the
fall of 1945, outlined briefly above, was fourfold. First,
unions were organized in Japan's three leading papers and
in many lesser ones. Second, managements were reorganized
in many instances in accordance with the demands of the
employees. Third, employees geined a theretofore unheard
of degree of participation in the conduct of manegement,
including the formulat:on of editorial policy. Fourth,
through company executives chosen by union dictation, em-
ployees occasionally controlled financial resources of
firms with disregard of the rights of the firms' legal
owners (stockholders).

Concurrently, the press manifested an increesing will-
ingness or desire to expose unrevealed facts about the war
and the role of wartime leaders; to criticize the institu-
tions that had facilitated Japan's going to war; and to dis-
cuss the steps necessary to reorient the nation.16
It would be hasty to conclude that the reorganizations
were solely responsible for the new trends in newspeper
practice; yet it is probeble that they played en importent
role.
The methods by which these results were won, however,
were more questionable. The sens of vulnerability of the
owners and executives of the Asahi and Mainichi es virtime
leaders undoubtedly cleared the path for the exertion of
employee pressure toward reorganization. In these two
cases, the changes of management were effected without re-
sistance, though their legality never has been subject to
a court decision. The Yomiuri's reorganization wes at
least partly the result of seizure of the plant by the em-
ployees in contravention of the legel rights of the owner.
Detention of the president as a war crimes suspect enabled
the employees' union to secure a contractural right to
shere in the functions of menegement.
EXTENSION OF POVER DURING 1946
The year 1946 witnessed an extension of the influence
of newspaper employee unions, both in securing better vages
and working conditions and in participating in menagement
functions, including editorial policy determination. In
the latter category, the two outstanding cases were those
of the Yomiuri and of the Hokkaido Shimbun.
The management council of the Mainichi, and that
formed in the Asahi in March 1946, apparently functioned
smoothly enough to avoid open collisions on policy. The
Mainichi employees' union, or at least printing shop mem-
bers of the union, in cne instence exercised a kind of
editorial control. Mimpo, a fled ling paper at the time,

was printed on the presses of the Mainichi. On 24 June
the Mimpo edition carried a cericature of the Emperor that
was in keeping with the editor's anti-Imperial policy.
When the press run was finished, the Mainichi workers re-

40,O00 copies, because, they said, "It is opposed to the

Cooler heeds in the Meinichi union sought without avail
to persuade the printshop members to release the remain-
ing, unburned copies of Mimpo. Occupation authorities
admonished the workers that they were denying Mimpo free-
dom of the press. The remaining copies, however, were not
delivered to Mimpo until early in July.18
Both the Hokkaido Shimbun and Yomiuri cases repre-
sented a more extensive and fundamental threat to the
freedom of the press than did the Meinichi incident.
Briefly, the story of the Hokkaido Shimbun follows.19
In February 1946, five employees of the Hokkaido Shimbun,
the only paper then published in Hokkaido, appeared in
Tokyo and related to SCiP that the editor-in-chief had
ordered all subeditors, reporters, end compositors to
join the politiccl party (Communist) to which he belonged,
or suffer discharge. SCAP told them that such a practice
would be not only undemocratic but also contrery to direc-
tives.
The directors of the Hokkaido Shimbun appointed a
new editor-in-chief. Although no further complaints of
politicel dictetion to the employees were heard, the edi-
torial policy end news coverage were so extreme and
biased as to incur the anger of the populace. SCAP in-
vestigation in June 1946 disclosed that the newspaper was
guilty of irresponsible journalism on a number of counts,
chief of which was violation of ell of the Press Code.
The fault lay vith the editor-in-chief, a member of the

The Yomiuri dispute wes more complicated.20 It grew
out of the dissipation of editorial responsibility that
resulted, in this case, from the institution of the manage-
ment council. In the six months following the agreement
of December 1945, Chief Editor Tomin SUZUKI, who wes also
chairman of the employees' union and a member of the
management council, gained more control over the editoriel
policy of the Yomiuri then President BABA. SUZUKI gener-
ally wes recognized to be a Communist sympathizer,21 and
under his direction the Yomiuri became so leftist in tone
that the circulation declined. Moreover, the paper was
guilty on numerous occasions of ineccurate reporting.
BABA and SUZUKI disagreed both on editoriel policy and on
means of ensuring accurate news reports. Their disegree-
ment came to a head in June 1946, in the wake of SCAP ad-
monitions over two particularly flagrant violations of the
Press Code,

RECENT TRENDS
The foregoing outlines the. high points of the ectivi-
ties of labor organizations in the press durirg the first
14 months of the Occupation. Some such beckground is
necessary to a more deteiled discussion of recent trends
and the present status of these org:nizetions.
Since the feilure of the "general strike" of press
and radio in October 1946, two primary trends stend out
in cases observed and reported. First, press unions in-
dividually have continued and even accelerated the process
of reeching working relationships with manegement through
contracts and regular procedures. Second, they have gone
fer to reject extremist leadership and policies, both in-
dividually and in their national union. The two tronds,
of course, are interlocked. In general, indivicuel un-
lons seem to have achieved the soundest growth and work-
ing relationships in proportion as they heve reject d ex-
tremism. In the national union, this process wes sherply
defined and is treated seperately, as is the cese of the
Proadcesting Corporation of Japan.
DEVELOPMENT OF WORKING RELATIONSIIPS
The failure of the October 1946 strike seemed to cc-
celerate, if anything, the process of establishing work-
ing relationships between press companies and their unions.
There eppeered to be steady progress towerd better-defined
contractual relationships and toward the use of consulte-
tive mechinery for the peeceful resolution of labor-men-
agement issues. At the same time, there were indicetions
of & growing understanding among th rank and file of un-
ion members of the concept :nd proper functions of a un-

The Mainichi union43 appears to have gone through more
vicissitudes then that of the Asahi in developing toward
the relative stability of the present. The union's exe-
cutive committee has changed four times since October 1946.
Although the Mainichi has had e contract since 22 April
1946, these changes of leadership appear to reflect both a
working out of labor-manegement reletions and a contest for
power within the union itself.
The first of these changes followed closely, as in the
Asahi, on the failure of the 5 October "general strike."44
A new executive committee, elected 16 October, lasted only
a short time. Its chief achievement was to take the
Mainichi union formally out of the general strike, but it
resigned, partly as a result of criticism by leftist ele-
ments within the union,45 and partly as a result of dif-
ferences among the Meinichi union branches.46
The next executive committee foundered on a wage in-
crease question. As the cheirman favored the figure that
the company was willing to grant,47 he was criticized by
leftists among the union members. The company's figure
wes finally accepted, but the executive committee resigned
on 25 January 1947.

71. According to Article IV of its constitution, the

SOME OBSERVATIONS ON PRESS UNTONS
Japan's press unions range from the 5,815-member or-
genizetion in the Asahi to the 65-member organizetion in
the Yuken Kyoto. Generel observations are difficult, be-
cause every case is different; but it eppears that in.
generel the larger unions have the greetest substance and
understanding of their function. The unions of the Asahi,
Meinichi, and Yomiuri, like the pepers themselves, are in
a separate class in respect to size, influerce, and degree
of orgenizetion.115 A second level of unions might be
considered to include those in the 500-1,500 member cate-
gory as in the Kyodo News Agency; Jiji Press; Tokyo Shim-
bun, Nippon Keizai, Jiji Shimpo; and in the leeding pro-
vincial papers--Chubu Nippon (Negoya), Hokkaido Shimbun,
and Nishi Nippon Shimbun (Fukuoka) and Kahoku Shimbun
(Sendai).
The remeinder, in generel, do not eppeer to pley en
importent role either within their own compenies or in
the AJNRWU. In older papers, Tike the Kobe Shimbun, re-
letionships tend to be vell-esteblished; in post surrender
pepers, like Miyako Shimbun (Kyoto), the staffs are likely
to be too small and too much engrossed in meking their
venture succeed to provide fruitful soil for the union
menegement.
The following observations, therefore, apply chiefly
to those unions with more then 500 members,

With the vertical form of orgenizetion, however, the
unions tend to operate disjointedly--that is, by groups
of members in the departments, sections, or sub-sections
in which they are located functionally. In the big three
and the BCJ, general meetings of the entire membership are
virtuelly impossible, and in the middle strεta of unions
they ere difficult.119
Therefore the theory and practice of representation
assume considerable importance. To teke probebly the
most refined exemple--the Asahi--the closest represente-
tion is found in the generel assembly of eech brench,120
where each delepate represents five union members. Since
the delegate is selected by colleagues in his functional
unit of the newspaper, emphasis is placed upon the in-
terests and attitudes of the employees in terms of their
functionel location in the compeny. This proportional
representstion of the verious units of the company's 'or-
ganization is carried up into the brench committee, where
each delegete represents 25 members; into the general
assembly of the entire Asahi union, where each delegete
represents 30 members; and into the centrel committee,
where each represents 300 members.121 The executive
mechinery, consisting of branch executive committees and
a central executive committee, does not carry functionel
representation to the same degree as does the "legislstive"
machinery, but does have the tendency. As & result, the
entire Asahi union, like other press unions, tends to be
divided into threc mein groups--editorial, business, and
mechenical employees.

CHIEF UNION-MANAGEMENT ISSUES
The chief questions at issue between press unions and
managements eppeer to be the standard questions of wages,
workine conditions, and job security. These questions well
may prove more and more difficult to resolve until Jepan's
overall economic conditions improve, for they are enmeshed
with infletion, the newsprint shortege, and the surplus of
manpower. Infietion drives salaried men to seek more pay
and benefits; the newsprint shortege mekes it difficult for
management to justify the employment of normal staffs, let
alone former employees repetrieted since the surrender; and
the surplus of menpower makes unions and manegements alike
reluctant to retionalize staff to needs. Therefore, man-
agements have in general conceded union wage demands, re-
tained unnecessarily large steffs, and soucht relief in in-
creased subscription prices, in side-line ectivities, end
in bank loans.
There is no law that forbids the discharge of surplus
personnel.134 It would undoubtedly be difficult to obtain
union consent to a lerge-scale lay-off plan.135 However,
134. Article ii of the Trade Union Law of 21 December 1945
forbids the employers to "discharge or give dis-
criminatory treatment to & worker for his being a
member of a trade union, for having tried to form
or join a trade union, or for having performed proper
acts of a trade union." This would enjoin discherpes
that aimed to break a union.

There follows a summary of the main points made by Mr. Killan,
Chief of SCAP's GHa Labor Division, in his discussion of the
Japanese labor situation:
1. Coal shortage
"Most" of Japan's econonic ills can be traced back to the
shortage of coal. The coal shortage in turn is largely the re-
sult of inexperienced and ill-fed labor, lack of mining equip-
ment ans a elleral deterioration of mines prought about by wartime
exploitation. Before the war most Japanese mines were operated
by Korean and Chinese "slave" laborers who worked a 6o-hour week
and for whom there were no safety siandards. In contrast, today
newly recruited free miners enjoy a 48-hour week and are pro-
C
tected py a comprehensive Lator Standards Act. The experience
S
or sCAP has been that the 8-hour day produces maximum efficiency,
particularly under the prevailine primitive mining conditions.
Althou h the ue ree of apsenteeism in the mines is disturbing,
there is no wiuespread unrest. In fact labor relations in the
1
coal mines have, sirce the war, been so ood that SCAp's Labor
Division undertook to iave incorporated In the Coal Nationali-
1
zation Bill measures to protect existine collective bargaining
procedur's. (Comment: Recallin that 3 of the 8 hours are
DCR - ITP Unit
NOV1
Anal.
FILED
spent
Rev
RESTHI CTED
Cat.
Dist

=== Page 282 === (confidence 0.98)
RESTRICTED
-2-
spent in getting to and from the pit-face and that a slightly
greater number of miners today are producing only 30 per cent
of 1936 levels, one is led to suspect that labor productivity
may have suffered considerably under the impact of trade union-
ism -- a suspicion not entirely removed by ir. Killan's emphasis
on the deterioration of mining equipment, etc.)
2. Surplus labor
The traditional Japanese concept of family persists in
industry. Once on the payroll, the worker is regarded as a
member of the company's family. Even if the company 1s opera-
ting on a reduced scale, due to war damage or lack of raw mate-
ri Is, there has been a tendency on the part of employers to
retain war workers as well as ex-employee returned servicemen.
Against this back round there has been considerable agi-
tation for rationalizin the surplus labor force on the grounds
that it has encourated inefficiency, raised costs and has pre-
judiced the Japanese competitive position in overseas markets.
However, 3CAP has opposed any large scale rationalization program
because: 1) as long as the workers are employed they are pro-
ducing something, however small; 2) the Japanese Government has
inadequate relief forces ano funds to take care of any large num-
ber of unemployed; and, 3) the Japanese Government is not strong
enouch to withstand the political consequences of such a ration-
alization prograu. SCapis policy will continue to be one of
encouraging the gradual shift of workers to jobs where they are
Lost needed.
3. Stabilization
Since setting the average composite wage at l8oo yen a
month, the index of living has risen 25 per cent and 1s likely
to continue upward as a result of the supplementary budget. It
will trerefore be impossible for the Government to resist a grow-
in worker demand that the wage scale be adjusted. SCAp's Labor
Division has suggested the formula that wage increases be based
on production increases with price ceilings being maintained.
4. Communist infiltration in trade unions
The Communist Party definitely controls the electrical
workers' union and has made considerable progress in penetrating
into the government transport and communications unions.
5. Future

Future of trade unionism in Japan
Mr. Killan spoke with evident conviction along the lines
that trade unionism in Japan is only beginning to take bold and
that, if the U.S. were to withdraw its control of Japan within
the next year or two trade unionism would be rapidly supplanted
by traditional Japanese labor practices. Speaking more gener-
ally, Mr. Killan expressed the opinion that unless the U.S. re-
tained direct control of Japan for at least another four or five
years, our efforts to bring about a democratic Japan would neces-
sarily fail. (Mr. Killan is presumably unaware of the current
thinking in the Department on post-treaty controls over Japan.)

The Acting Political Adviser has the honor to enclose five

dated December 19, 1947, informing the Japanese Government that
there is a shortage of laborers needed to complete Occupation
projects and that the shortage is attributed to inefficient
distribution of food and clothing rations, lack of transportation,
844-015
and financial difficulties of contractors due to delay in payments.
The Japanese Government is instructed to take remedial measures by
making prompt payments in accordance with contracts, properly
supervising equitable distribution of food and clothing rations,
and furnishing adequate transportation.
IPED

SUBJECT: Text of a Memorandum Believed to Have Been
Prepared by Elements of the Japan Communist.
Party.
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
894.504/1-648
19
The Honorable
005
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to enclose copy of the text (in
translation and Japanese) of an undated memorandum which
is believed to have been prepared by certain elements of
the Japan Communist Party. The paper, which contains
the minutes of two meetings of a conference of the
"National Fraction of Coal Miners" (held on October 4
and October 12, 1947, respectively), was presented to me
informally by Mr. ESAKU Sato, Vice Minister of the
Japanese Ministry of Transportation, as evidence that
IR
the Japan Communist Party has agents in labor unions and
government positions.
From the memorandum it may be seen that Communist
elements are endeavoring to encourage labor strife, with
the obvious purpose of delaying the rehabilitation of
the Japanese economy and preventing increased production.
This purpose is revealed by the memorandum in which the
"really serious" economic crisis in Japan is welcomed as
CS/A
"helpful to the Party combat". Such crisis is desired,
not only because it furnishes conditions favorable to ex-
pansion of the Japan Communist Party's strength, but also
王要
because development of economic chaos would discredit the
policies of the Occupation and simultaneously those of the
United States. (That criticism of Occupation policy is
considered another means of attacking United States for-
eign policy is evidenced by the manner in which Soviet
propaganda organs link the two by use of such terms as
"American Occupation authorities".)
That discrediting the Occupation is an objective of 
the Soviet Government has already been amply demonstrated
by the manner in which the Soviet Member of the Allied
Council for Japan has distorted facts. As the Departments
194864
is aware, he has, at meetings of the Council, repeatedly
made
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 287 === (confidence 1.00)
ORIGINAL of Enclosure to Despatch No. 13
dated January 6, 1948 from the Office of
the United States Political Adviser for
Japan, Tokyo, entitled "Text of a
Memorandum Believed to Have Been Pre-
pared by Elements of the Japan Communist

=== Page 288 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's No.13
January 6, 1948
CONFIDENTIAL
-2-
made statements critical of Occupation policies such as
those concerned with education, land reform, public health
measures, and the repatriation program. (In connection
with the latter, reference is made to this Mission's des-
patch No. 1471 of December 22, 1947, concerning the pro-
posed prosecution of a Communist Party newspaper for
violation of the "Press Code" and previous correspondence
concerning Communist misrepresentation of the repatriation
program of this Headquarters). The Communist line of the
"fraction" which circulated the enclosed memorandum is
clearly indicated by repetition of propaganda from Pravda
with regard to international affairs. For example, upon
the basis of an interpretation of an article from that
newspaper, the statement is made that "by a rapid growth
of the people's popular influence in Western Europe, the
Soviet voice in the United Nations is gaining power every
day".
A desire on the part of such Communist elements to
discredit the Occupation may further be implied from
criticism of policies commonly believed to be supported
by this Headquarters. For example, rationalization of
industry, anti-monopoly measures, and the "Temporary
Agricultural Production Adjustment Law" are each attacked
as part of an "intrigue" to orient the Japanese economy
toward that of a foreign colony by subsidizing exports
through "half-slavery" wages. In addition, the implication
that the withholding tax (collection of which has received
favorable comment from officials of this Headquarters) has
resulted in collection of taxes from labor only, is cal-
culated to cause resentment on the part of employees and
may contribute to tax delinquency.  The abolition of income
taxes, also recommended in the memorandum, would have the
effect of preventing the financing of reform programs
recommended by this Headquarters. Effectuation of such
recommendations would also have serious effects upon the
delicate balance of the Japanese Government's financial
stability.
A familiar Communist method of attack may be noted in
the recommendation that "local struggle and...workshop
battles" be used as an election weapon. It is believed
that the use of local disturbances rather than a general
strike is advised as a means of avoiding repetition of an
order such as that issued by General MacArthur on January
31, 1947 prohibiting a proposed general strike.
Of incidental interest is the recommendation contained
in the memorandum that support be given to the affiliation
of the National Coal Miners Union (Zen Tan) with the National
Congress of Industrial Organizations (Sambetsu) an important
labor organization in which Communists are believed to be
influential.

=== Page 300 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
Enclosure to Despatch No. 13 dated
January 6, 1947, from the Office of
the United States Political Adviser
for Japan, Tokyo, entitled "Text of
a Memorandum Believed to Have Been
Prepared by Elements of the Japan
Communist Party".
TRANSLATION
I. On September 2l, the Tokyo Headquarters of the
Party called a conference of the"Central Fraction of
Coal Miners". This conference was attended by the
representatives of local fractions then visiting Tokyo
the Party Headquarters personnel and the fraction of the
National Congress of Industrial Organizations (Sambetsu),
and a combat policy was decided upon with the wage question
as its center. On October 4 a conference of the National
Fraction of Coal Miners"was called, taking advantage of the
opportunity of the presence in Tokyo of representatives who
were attending the general meetings of the National Coal
Miners Union (Zen Tan) and the National Council of Coal
Miners Unions (Tan Kyo Zenkoku). The following combat
policy was adopted on the basis of the decision made on
September 21:
II. The local fractions are to take vigorous
action to establish a concrete plan on the basis
of this decision according to local conditions;
obtain approval of the local fraction organs; and
report their decision to Party Headquarters, the
Leadership Department of Organized Activities,
and the Coal Mine Fraction.
MINUTES (Conference of the National
Fraction of Coal Miners, October 4,1947)
I. Report on international and domestic conditions:
(a) International Situation.
As manifested in an article contributed by a Soviet
economist "Valga" (or Varga) to the Pravda Newspaper, the
economic crisis in America is spreading, Britain has
closed the sterling block, and the Marshall economic policy
in Europe has met decisive difficulties. By a rapid growth
of the people's democratic influence in mid-Western Europe,
the Soviet voice in the United Nations is gaining power
every day. This is proved by the fact that Soviet Russia
is striking the United States on the head at the latest
sessions of the United Nations. Following this growth of
the people's influence and the overwhelming victory of the
Chinese Communists, pressure upon Japan is gaining weight
proportionally, especially against the development of coal
mines as the Ruhr of the Far East--the foundation of the
Far East.
(b) Domestic Situation.
By the intrigue of financial monopolistic capitalists,
who are trying, by means of half-slavery wages, to orient
the Japanese
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 301 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to
Tokyo's 13
January 6, 1948.
CONFIDENTIAL
-2-
the Japanese economy toward foreign colonies by exports,
production in the wage-earning industries is being
forcibly carried out. The rationalization of industry,
maintenance of an 1,800 yen average monthly wage level,
opposition to concentrations of capital and the agricul-
tural production adjustment law--are all related to this
one point. This last mentioned ordinance is especially
calculated to establish legally the price differential
(Shiere?) for the purpose of exploiting the agricultural
and industrial laborers simultaneously. This, however,
results in the destruction of labor's power. The abandon-
ment of workshops by the laborers is the proof. According
to the Labor-Farmer News, the masses who are submitting
their demands at present are estimated to exceed 3,000,000,
or on a larger scale than at the time of the general strike
of February 1.
Furthermore, the supplementary budget which is pre-
sented to the present session of the Diet will reach 1l0
billion yen. This will raise prices beginning in the lat-
ter part of December. The Economic Stabilization Board
expects the economic condition in Japan in April-May next
year to be worse than the disaster in Germany after the
World War I. The note issue in Japan at the end of next
year will reach 400 to 500 billion yen. The economic
crisis in Japan is really serious. This situation is ad-
vantageous to our combat. There is no longer any other
solution than a revolutionary method. This time we must
fight fundamentally by commodity-exhausting guerrilla tac-
tics and by Baltizan strike tactics. A frontal attack upon
the strong defense line of the enemy is wasteful and dif-
ficult. We must develop our workshop struggle to an area
struggle and it is the cells of the Party which do this.
II. Wage Combat.
As the fundamental issue, the decision of September
21 is re-affirmed in an over-all way. We, however, must
not wage a separate battle for a winter-passing allowance,
a reduction of income tax, or for assuring safety devices;
each battle must be related to minimum wages. All efforts
should be made in parallel with the demand for minimum wages.
If a fight can be staged for minimum wages alone, it should
be pursued vigorously. We hereby re-affirm that we must
carry out a decisive and revolutionary battle by pushing for-
ward in thorough-going combat for the acquisition of minimum
wages.
In this sense, technically, the Central Coal Miners
Council (Chuo Tan Kyo) should develop a many-angled fight
according to the conditions in the coal mines, by means of
the following demands:
(1) the
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 302 === (confidence 1.00)
Enclosure to
Tokyo's No.13
January 6, 1948
CONFIDENTIAL
-3-
(1) the establishment of minimum wages; (2)
abolition of income tax; (3) introduction of the
retirement allowance; (4) miners' allowance; (5)
completion of safety devices.
In each mine and in each locality the following demands
should be presented:
(1) payment of a winter-passing bonus; (2)
payment of income tax by the company; (3) completion
of safety equipment.
It is clear that the Federation of Coal Miners' Unions
(Tan Ren) is trying to split the organization of the Coal
Miners Council (Tan Kyo) on the question of the wage demand.
What should we do then?
(1) There are two wage plans: one for 2,400 calories
of food monthly; and another for 2,200 calories advanced
by the Federation of Coal Miners' Unions. We must advance
with one demand for wages, grasping firmly upon the wage
plan, as our cells are not yet in a position to exercise
influence upon the masses.  For the latter reason, fractions
must lead the cells in such a manner as to facilitate their
activities in the easiest way. For this purpose, we must
gain time to transmit two wage plans to the lower parts of
the workers' organizations. Through this process, we can
capture the support of neutral union members, and inflict a
blow upon the leaders of the Federation of Coal Miners' Union.
(2) If the Federation of Coal Miners' Union outdoes us
in spite of this, we will stick to a one-way demand, laying
stress upon gaining time. In a word, in the present revolution-
ary stage we must fight out the minimum wage struggle.
In the case of 1 above, Hokkaido should fight for the
skill-encouraging allowance and for an area wage demand. Ef-
forts should be exerted to hold a general meeting in Hokkaido
again, and even if such a meeting is not possible, Hokkaido
should fight by itself.
III. Combat Through National Coal Control Law.
National coal control should be combined with our combat
as a question that affects the masses. In other words, such
matters as the completion of safety devices, welfare facili-
ties, payment of retirement allowance, the acquisition of
materials for increasing production, etc., are made possible
only through national control of coal mines. Such is es-
pecially true with small mines. This fact should propagandized
as a means of combat. Technically the absolute plan should be
avoided, and the secondary plan adopted (people's control
should be recognized in practice).
Further, the Liberal Party capitalists should be at-
tacked, and the Social Democratic Party bureaucrats supported.
In expediting national coal control, care must be taken not to
give
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 303 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to
Tokyo's No. 13
January 6, 1948
CONFIDENTIAL
-4-
give an impression to the masses that the Social Democratic
Party is right.
In short, we must never tamper with the law; we must
relate national coal control to our production combat, and
lead up to a wage struggle.
IV. Combat of Opposition to Taxation of the Masses.
At present there is collected only 20% of all taxes
imposed on the masses (mostly business income tax) except
the income tax on labor. Only the income tax on labor is
collected l00%, as salaries and wages are paid by the com-
pany less income tax, and the companies do not turn over
all tax revenues collected. If the supplementary budget of
ll0 billion yen is passed, four times the increased income
tax and nine times the old income tax will be collected.
90% of the business budget this year (amounting to 114.5
billion yen) represents taxes on the masses. As detailed
data will be presented by our fraction in the Finance Ministry,
concrete figures will be exposed for the purpose of our wage
struggle. We must form a League for Opposition to Taxes and
join hands with the farms and city-workers. Thus we must
pursue the following steps:
(1) as a matter of principle, abolition/of
income tax7, rather than delay, must be stressed;
(2) concretely, technical operations are
adopted, such as delaying payment until workers'
living expenses are in the black, or making em-
ployers bear the income tax;
(3) it is advantageous to know that the
party responsible for the payment of tax (income
tax) is the employer, and an employee is not
legally bound for delay or non-payment.
V. Fight for Increased Production and Policy
Toward the Coal Rehabilitation Council.
In order to avoid the oppression of the authorities,
the combat for completing safety devices, decided upon
September 2l, should not be displayed prominently.  Safety
devices themselves constitute a subject for combat. This
means that a bona fide battle should be fought to rejeet
oppression, and that a combat is to be staged not only by
the chief officials of the Party but also by the masses.
We must destroy the false illusion that the economy
can be rehabilitated through the Economic Rehabilitation
Council or Coal Rehabilitation Council. At present,
conditions are ripe to expose the paternalistic character
of the Coal Rehabilitation Council and to crush all proposals
made by the capitalist side. We must carefully examine our
subjective conditions, re-examine the weaknesses of the two
Councils, and work for the formation of a labor front through
the Coal Miners Council.
Before
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 304 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to
Tokyo's No.13
January 6, 1948
CONFIDENTIAL
-5-
Before we mention the shift system, we must expose
how intensive labor is imposed on the workers by feudalis-
tic low wages, and accuse the capitalists of production
sabotage. Our combat for opening up the bottleneck in the
production of 30,000,000 tons of coal is combined with the
state coal control battle and our wage struggle. Naturally,
we insist on four shifts of six hours each, and oppose the
shifting of positions. Careful consideration should be
given before coming to a conclusion in favor of the employ-
ment of new workers. An example is furnished by the Takahagi
Branch of the National Coal Miners Union (Zen Tan) which de-
mands a two shift system of six hours each.
VI. Policy of Organized Activities.
We should cast off the false mechanical idea of the
past that the Coal Miners Council (Tan Kyo) is the founda-
tion upon which unity of the coal miners is established.
We have to bring forward our fundamental policy of building
unity through common struggle, and a single national union
should be established even if we abandon those who drop out.
A common combat committee should be formed in each locality,
and a national common combat committee should also be formed.
In this sense, it is a wrong policy to keep forever the
faction of the National Coal Miners Union (Zen Tan) in the
Saga Prefectural Coal Miners Association (Tan Kyo). Re-
jecting oppression, and developing obstinately local strug-
gles by repeating powerful and flexible workshop battles, we
should tie up these battles with the next election campaign.
The fractions, with cells as the center, should create
advantageous conditions, however slight, for the purpose of
winning a decisive revolutionary battle to be fought in co-
operation with the farms and city workers, for increased
wages and complete installation of safety devices, and
against taxation of the masses.
Minutes of a Conference of the National
Fraction of Coal Miners (0ctober 12, 1947)
I. As reported by bourgeois papers, a great majority
of the Japan Miners Union (Nikko) and the Federation of Coal
Miners' Union (Tan Ren) walked out of the general meeting of
the Coal Miners Council (Tan Kyo) which was convened on
October 12. In order to discuss future combat policy in the
face of this new split, a meeting of the National Fraction
of Coal Miners was called on the night of October 12, with
the presence, by invitation, of two central committee members
of Party Headquarters. The betrayal of the Japan Miners
Union (Nikko) and the Federation of Coal Miners' Unions (Tan
Ren) will be reported in detail in the press organ of the
National Coal Miners Union (Zen Tar); it is recommended that
positive action should be taken on the basis thereof.
Minutes
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 305 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to
Tokyots No. 13
January 6, 1948
CONFIDENTIAL
-6-
Minutes
The following three items were chiefly discussed as
future policy; (1) counter measures toward labor unity;
(2) policy toward the new movement of the National Coal
Miners Union (Zen Tan); (3) policy of combat.
As a result the following decisions were adopted:
(1) By means of thorough propaganda, a true report
concerning the general meeting should be made known and
the responsibility for setting our combat back exposed;
with this as a pre-supposition, a fight for the acquisition
of minimum wages in each workshop and in each locality,
with 2,400 calories as the base is clearly indicated;
fighting should begin in the area where the Union is strong
and be expanded to other areas;
(2) The unity of the remaining unions must be con-
solidated as speedily as possible; for this purpose, those
remaining in the National Coal Miners Union (Zen Tan) should
be unified and union regulations discarded;
(3) In short, the National Coal Miners Union has in-
creased to 220,000 members in practice. The opportunity
must not be neglected of cutting into the masses who seceded
from the general meeting and of absorbing these elements
successfully. In other words, the remaining unions should
be unified, and the seceding unions dragged into a common
combat;
(4) The present stage has been reached through common
battle, by split, and by unification into a single body.
Therefore, the policy pursued up to this time of abandoning
those who fell out of the common struggle, has now been
realized.
(5) Question with regard to National Congress of
Industrial Organizations (Sambetsu).
This question comes up only when unification is com-
pleted, or when the National Coal Miners Union (Zen Tan)
discards its regulations. As long as the organization of
the National Coal Miners Union exists, the question con-
cerning the National Congress of Industrial Organizations
cannot arise. Naturally, at the time when decisions are
taken by mass debates at the inaugural meeting of such an
organization, the National Coal Miners Union should pro-
pagandize its fundamental policy most energetically, and
propose affiliation with the National Congress of Industrial
Organizations.
(6) The Federation of Coal Miners' Unions (Tan Ren)
and Japan Miners Union (Nikko) may come to an agreement on
the average wage question. In this case, we must uncon-
ditionally demand wages equivalent to those they have ac-
quired. In addition we must demand minimum wages. Even
when
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 306 === (confidence 0.97)
Enclosure to
Tokyo's No.13
January 6, 1948
CONFIDENTIAL
-7-
when they begin negotiations for an average wage, we must
undertake collective bargaining separately and most power-
fully.

SUBJECT:
Shortage of Japanese Laborers
1. Reference your letter, C. L. O. No. 9917 (AG), subject as
above, dated 30 December 1947, which forwarded initial report of
progress required by paragraph 3, SCAPIN 1832.
2. The labor situation has not yet improved sufficiently, and
further vigorous action is necessary, particularly in regard to the
distribution of food and clothing rations to laborers. It is desired
that such action be taken without delay.
3. An additional report of progress (in duplicate) is desired
not later than 10 February 1948 and thereafter at ten day intervals
until further advice from General Headquarters.

SUBJECT:
Japan: Suggested Decentralization of Administrative
Control over Labor Systems and Programs
Information received in this Division from Japan
indicates that the Labor Division, ESS, SCAP, is under
some pressure to agree to a move involving the transfer
Centr
RM/R
Inspection System and the mployment Security (Employment
Service) Pro ram from the  ational Government of Japan to 6
the governments of the various Prefectures. This pressure
cent dust Be Returned
has grown out of administrative changes contemplated as
a result of the recent passage of the Local Autonomy Law 5
by the Diet. Our information does not state whether the O
source or this pressure is from other Sections or Division
within SCaP or from the Japanese Government. Whichever
the case may be, the Labor Division is concerned and has
1
infornally requested that we forward to them material
they can use in maintainin their position that decen
30
tralization or thes particular administrative functions
would be undesirable. In view of the importance of 
this issue, however, we would like to inform you at 
this time of the views of this Division on the matter
in question.
CSE
We believe that decentralization or administrative
control in the fields of labor standards inspection and
BM
employment service programs would be unwise in that it
would move in a direc ion contrary to that dictated by
past experlence to be necessary for the efficient oper-
ation of such systems. International practice, as seen
in various Conventions and Recommendations or the
International Labour crganization, also supports this
position. The ollowin examples typify the fLo position
in regard to this problem.
A proposed convention of the International Labor
Organization, to be submitted to the Thirty-First
Session
894.50
4
8-h

=== Page 311 === (confidence 0.98)
Session in San Francisco in June of this year, stat
"The employment service shall consist of
a national systen or employment offices under the
control of a central authority." (artiole 2)
The drafting committee for this proposed convention,
consisting of 60 mewbers (30 government, 15 employers',
15 workers') representing many different countries
adopted this articl: without discussion. a step which
bears witness to the universality or the acceptance of
the soundness of this administrative practice.
Another convention or the ILo, concerning Labour
Inspections in Industry ani commerce adopted in 1947
states:
1. "So rar as is compatible with
Administrative practice or a He.iber, labour
inspection shali be placed under the super-
vision and control or a central authority."
(Article 4)
The qualiried language here, it should be pointed out,
is the result not of any question as to the desirability
of centralized control of the labor inspection function
but of the recognition of the practical impossibility
of securing a shift in the existing control systom in
countries such as the United States where such functions
are historically decentralized.
We are bringing this matter to your attention in
case this issue either comes to you for consideration
or the Department at any time in the near future reviows
policy in connection with the general problem of dooen-
tralization of Government powers in Japan. In either
case we would appreciate consideration being given to
the viewpoint of this Division in regard to the admin-
istrative arrangements for these two labor laws, and we
would be glad to discuss the matter in greater detail
with you at any time.

Sir:
have the honor to enclose copies of a memorandum dated January 21,
1948 concerning a conversation (which is summarized below) with YONEkUBO
ATION
Mitsusuke, Minister of Labor; YOSHITAKE Eiichi, Vice Minister of Labor;
LH
KATSURA Takashi, Acting Chairman, Central Labor Committee; AYUSAWA Iwao,
Secretary-General of the Central Labor Committee; and two officers of this
FE
Mission. Subjects discussed were efficiency of Japanese labor; national-
ization of governmental administrative personnel; responsibilities of
Euklx
governmental agencies and the Central Labor Committee under Japanese labor
DR
laws; the Emperor and his trips through Japan; and encouragement of eco-
nomic disruption by Conmunists and ultra-conservatives.
Summary. Minister Yonekubo stated that present Japanese labor
legislation is equal to the best in the world, but has a long dis-
tance to go before being fully implemented. He stressed that lack
of'effietency of Japanese labor is a major hurdle and that there is
urgent ne'ed to awaken labor to its duties and responsibilities as
otherwise, he feels, raw materials and financial aid will not be
attracted to Japan.
Mr. Yoshitake said that a cut of 25% will be made among govern-
mntal administrative employees. However, since most governmental
agencies have less than their authorized personnel, this reduction
will reguire dismissal of only 5% of those presently employed.
Minister Yonekubo indicated that a bill similar to the Taft-
Hartley Labor Act had been proposed in Cabinet discussions but that
he strongly opposed it. He believes Japan's labor laws should be
amended to define more specifically the terms "dispute" and "act
of dispute". Mr. Katsura and Dr. Ayusawa intimated that Cabinet
Ministers have not taken full advantage of their statutory authority
to discipline employees. Mr. Yoshitake pointed out that labor union
officials usually refuse to accept responsibility for labor disorders.
Mr. Katsura observed that both communists and ultra-conservatives
welcome economic disruption as a means of undermining democratic
reform. Minister Yonekubo singled out the National Congress of
FILD
Industrial Organizations (Sambetsu) as strongly influenced by com
munists. He said communists are now shifting concentration of their
disruptive efforts from coal to transportation.

SUBJCT:
Submission of Monthly Labor Renort
1. References:
